368	
369	\paragraph{TOST equivalence testing.}\label{app:tost}
370	The primary evidence against selection gains comes from effect sizes and confidence intervals: Cohen's $d{=}0.038$ for the best signal (NER LP) and bootstrap 95\% CI [$-$3.0, $+$4.2]~pp spanning zero.
371	Table~\ref{tab:tost} supplements this with two one-sided tests (TOST), testing whether each signal's selection F1 is equivalent to single-sample within a bound $\delta$.
372	The $\delta$ values are contextualized against oracle headroom (12--18~pp): $\delta{=}5$~pp corresponds to forgoing 28--38\% of the available headroom, $\delta{=}3$~pp forgoing 17--23\%, and $\delta{=}2$~pp less than 15\%.
373	These proportions use the same test data that defines the headroom, introducing circularity: $\delta$ is anchored to the very quantity it contextualizes.
374	We address this below with externally anchored equivalence margins.
375	The breakpoint $\delta^*$, the minimum equivalence margin at which each signal passes TOST, ranges from approximately 1.3~pp (SJ, ${\sim}$10\% of oracle headroom) to 3.4~pp (FK, ${\sim}$26\%) on NER, and from approximately 2.9~pp (LP, ${\sim}$16\%) to 6.5~pp (VC, ${\sim}$36\%) on RE.
376	On NER ($n{=}529$), at $\delta{=}{\pm}2$~pp, SJ and LP are confirmed equivalent to single-sample (TOST $p{<}0.05$); at $\delta{=}{\pm}3$~pp, four of five signals pass; at $\delta{=}{\pm}5$~pp, all five pass.
377	On RE ($n{=}397$, preliminary), EM and VC fail equivalence even at $\delta{=}{\pm}5$~pp due to systematic underperformance ($\Delta{=}{-}3.2$~pp and $-4.5$~pp respectively).
378	The effect sizes are negligible ($d{=}0.038$) and confidence intervals span zero, with TOST confirming equivalence at practically meaningful margins.
379	
380	\begin{table}[t]
381	\centering
382	\small
383	\caption{TOST equivalence testing: signals vs.\ single-sample on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). \checkmark: equivalent at $p{<}0.05$; \texttimes: not equivalent. $\Delta$: mean F1 difference (signal $-$ single-sample).}
384	\label{tab:tost}
385	\begin{tabular}{l cccc cccc}
386	\toprule
387	& \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{NER}} & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{RE}} \\
388	\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-9}
389	Signal & $\Delta$ & $\pm$2 & $\pm$3 & $\pm$5 & $\Delta$ & $\pm$2 & $\pm$3 & $\pm$5 \\
390	\midrule
391	LP & +0.6 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$0.9 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
392	SJ & +0.1 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$1.8 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark \\
393	FK & $-$0.5 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$1.6 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark \\
394	EM & $-$0.8 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$3.2 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \texttimes \\
395	VC & $-$1.0 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark & $-$4.5 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \texttimes \\
396	\bottomrule
397	\end{tabular}
398	\end{table}
399	
400	\paragraph{External equivalence margins.}
401	The oracle-headroom anchors above introduce circularity: $\delta$ is calibrated against the very quantity it contextualizes.
402	To resolve this, we supplement with three externally grounded margins:
403	(1) $\delta_{\mathrm{SEM}}$: the bootstrapped standard error of single-sample F1 (0.43--1.33~pp depending on configuration), which represents measurement noise;
404	(2) $\delta_{\mathrm{practical}}{=}0.5$~pp, the threshold below which NER differences are considered negligible \cite{ratinov2009design};
405	(3) $\delta_{\mathrm{literature}}{=}1.0$~pp, the approximate significant-difference threshold established in NER evaluation methodology \cite{bergkirkpatrick2012empirical}.
406	
407	Results under these external anchors diverge sharply by configuration.
408	On LLaMA CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), all signals pass equivalence at every $\delta$ ($p{<}0.001$), consistent with the near-zero selection deltas ($|\Delta|{\leq}0.1$~pp) and tight CIs at this sample size.
409	On Qwen CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), no signal passes at any external $\delta$: selection F1 degrades by 0.9--1.5~pp relative to single-sample, which exceeds even $\delta_{\mathrm{literature}}$.
410	On SciERC NER ($n{=}529$), only SJ passes at $\delta_{\mathrm{SEM}}{=}1.33$~pp (the widest SEM bound); all other signals and tighter margins fail.
411	These external anchors confirm that the equivalence conclusion is configuration-dependent: LLaMA CoNLL selection is robustly equivalent to single-sample under any reasonable margin, while SciERC and Qwen CoNLL selection shows detectable degradation at externally calibrated thresholds.
412	
413	\paragraph{Multiple comparison considerations.}\label{app:multiple_comparisons}
414	Our primary selection analysis involves ${\sim}$20 comparisons (5 signals $\times$ 3--4 configurations).
415	Since all primary selection results are non-significant ($p{>}0.05$), applying Benjamini-Hochberg correction can only make these results more conservative, strengthening the ``no signal beats greedy'' conclusion.
416	The sole positive finding, the non-tied LP subset ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}), survives Bonferroni correction over the 5 signal comparisons ($p_{\mathrm{adj}}{=}0.020 < 0.05$). However, the $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ threshold is selected post-hoc from a 7-point sweep (Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}); joint correction across thresholds and signals yields $p_{\mathrm{adj}}{=}0.14$, so this finding is exploratory.
417	For quality estimation signal rankings, differences between the top signals (e.g., VC vs.\ SJ on SciERC) should be interpreted as descriptive rather than as statistically confirmed orderings, given the limited power of 3-seed comparisons.
418	
419	\paragraph{When would consistency-based selection work?}\label{app:when_selection_works}
420	Our negative results are specific to fine-tuned models with constrained decoding on structured IE. Several conditions outside our experimental scope could restore selection headroom.
421	(a)~Models with poor calibration or in-context learning setups (without fine-tuning) produce more diverse outputs, widening the gap between candidates that consistency signals can exploit.
422	(b)~Closed-source APIs where log-probabilities are unavailable make consistency signals the only viable selection mechanism; our structural signals (SJ, VC, FK, EM) provide quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.45) even without model-internal access.
423	(c)~Ensemble or mixture-of-experts settings generate structurally diverse candidates by design, reducing instance degeneracy below the levels observed in our single-model sampling.
424	(d)~Tasks where the model exhibits systematic overconfidence (e.g., hallucination-prone generation) may benefit from consensus filtering, as disagreement among samples would carry more discriminative signal than in our well-calibrated fine-tuned regime.
425	
426	\section{Supervised and Model-Internal Signal Analysis}\label{app:supervised_combination}
427	
428	\subsection{Supervised Signal Combination}
429	
430	Supervised classifiers (logistic regression, MLP, random forest) trained on all five signals (5-fold CV, 3-seed mean, SciERC, $N{=}16$, $+17.9$~pp headroom) all perform at or below greedy (F1${=}0.644$; LR: $-0.4$~pp; MLP: $-1.6$~pp; RF: $-3.1$~pp).
431	Adding pairwise interaction features does not help (AUROC 0.634 vs.\ 0.638).
432	To test whether richer representations could close the gap, we train a DeBERTa-v3-base verifier that encodes input-output pairs and predicts per-sample F1.
433	DeBERTa achieves $\rho{=}0.329{\pm}0.087$ (5-fold CV, 3 seeds). Despite moderate quality prediction, selection utility is poor: on SciERC, DeBERTa closes $-26.5{\pm}26.5$\% of the oracle gap (13 of 15 runs negative; vs.\ LP's $+4.2{\pm}10.7$\%); on Few-NERD, DeBERTa recovers only $+3.5$\% (single 80/20 split; vs.\ LP's $+16.3$\%).
434	Cross-dataset transfer fails entirely ($-27$\% to $-72$\% gap closure).
435	Neither handcrafted signal combinations nor learned text representations close the selection gap; only model-internal log-probability provides meaningful selection signal, consistent with an information asymmetry between the generating model and external observers.
436	
437	\subsection{Hidden State Probes}\label{app:hidden_probe}
438	
439	The preceding negative results suggest the bottleneck is model-internal rather than a feature engineering problem.
440	We test this directly by training probes on the generating model's hidden representations.
441	
442	\paragraph{Setup.}
443	For each of $N{=}8$ samples per instance, we extract the mean-pooled final-layer hidden state ($d{=}4096$) from the generating Qwen3-8B model and train a linear probe and a two-layer MLP (hidden dim${=}256$) to predict per-sample F1 (5-fold CV, 3 seeds).
444	
445	\begin{table}[t]
446	\centering
447	\small
448	\caption{Hidden state probes vs.\ LP for sample selection (5-fold CV ${\times}$ 3 seeds). $\rho$: Spearman correlation with per-sample F1. Sel.\ F1: selection F1. Gap\%: oracle gap closure. SciERC greedy${=}0.644$, oracle${=}0.775$.}
449	\label{tab:hidden_probe}
450	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
451	\begin{tabular}{@{}l ccc cc@{}}
452	\toprule
453	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{SciERC}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Few-NERD}} \\
454	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
455	Method & $\rho$ & Sel.\ F1 & Gap\% & $\rho$ & Gap\% \\
456	\midrule
457	LP & .283 & $\mathbf{.650}$ & $+$4.8 & .606 & 12.3 \\
458	Hidden Linear & $.179{\pm}.019$ & $.613{\pm}.002$ & $-$23.4 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{---} \\
459	Hidden MLP & $\mathbf{.388{\pm}.013}$ & $.637{\pm}.003$ & $-$5.1 & $\mathbf{.629{\pm}.007}$ & 11.7 \\
460	\bottomrule
461	\end{tabular}}
462	\end{table}
463	
464	\paragraph{Cross-dataset asymmetry.}
465	On SciERC, the MLP probe achieves higher quality correlation than LP ($\rho{=}0.388$ vs.\ $0.283$, $+37$\%); hidden states encode information beyond what LP captures.
466	Yet higher correlation does not yield better selection: MLP selection F1 ($0.637$) falls below LP's ($0.650$), with gap closure turning negative ($-5.1$\% vs.\ $+4.8$\%; Table~\ref{tab:hidden_probe}).
467	The linear probe performs worse still ($\rho{=}0.179$, gap closure $-23.4$\%).
468	On Few-NERD, the MLP provides no increment over LP (gap closure $11.7$\% vs.\ $12.3$\%).
469	A learned token-level LP aggregator (1D CNN over per-token log-probabilities) matches mean LP on Few-NERD (gap closure $56.4$\% vs.\ $55.8$\%); LP's bottleneck is not token aggregation.
470	The correlation-selection gap extends to model-internal representations: in the high-capability regime (greedy F1${=}0.644$), inter-sample F1 differences are small enough that improved ranking provides no marginal selection value, consistent with the capability-diversity tension (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
471	
472	\section{Multi-Seed Stability}\label{app:multiseed}
473	
474	Table~\ref{tab:multiseed} reports quality estimation metrics across three random seeds for both model families on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).
475	On NER, the signal ranking VC${>}$SJ${>}$EM${>}$FK${>}$LP is identical across all six model-seed combinations, with $\sigma{\leq}0.025$ for all signals (rankings are stable across our 3 seeds, though statistical power with $n{=}3$ is limited).
476	On RE (Qwen only, preliminary), VC remains the top signal ($\rho{=}0.309{\pm}0.024$), but LP rises to \#2 ($\rho{=}0.297{\pm}0.022$), above FK, SJ, and EM. This reversal from the NER ranking reflects RE's higher sensitivity to per-sample scoring.
477	LLaMA absolute values are systematically lower than Qwen on NER, consistent with the directionally-consistent-but-magnitude-dependent characterization in the main text.

LP $\\rho{=}0.328$ may differ slightly from the registry value (0.315) due to instance filtering.} LLaMA SciERC (VC $\\rho{=}0.452$ vs.\\ LP 0.277, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.175$), and LLaMA CoNLL 3-seed (SJ $\\rho{=}0.474{\\pm}0.009$ vs.\\ LP $0.311{\\pm}0.003$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.163$).\n\n\\paragraph{FK independence: instance-level permutation test.}\\label{app:fk_independence}\n\nTo verify that Fleiss' $\\kappa$ reflects genuine within-instance agreement rather than marginal-rate artifacts, we conduct instance-level permutation tests across three configurations.\nFor each instance, we permute sample labels 10{,}000 times to generate a null distribution of FK under independence, then compare the observed FK against this null via a one-sample $t$-test and permutation $p$-value.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Instance-level FK independence test. Permutation tests confirm FK reflects true within-instance agreement (all $p{<}0.001$). Split-half reliability $r$ computed by randomly partitioning $N$ samples into two halves and correlating their FK scores.}\n\\label{tab:fk_independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrcccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $n$ & FK mean & 95\\% CI & $t$ & Split-half $r$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSciERC (Qwen) & 529 & 0.402 & [0.376, 0.428] & 30.8 & 0.395 \\\\\nCoNLL (LLaMA) & 2{,}756 & 0.927 & [0.919, 0.935] & 225.9 & 0.578 \\\\\nCoNLL (Qwen) & 2{,}756 & 0.720 & [0.706, 0.734] & 102.1 & 0.520 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:fk_independence} reports the results.\nAll configurations reject the independence null at $p{<}0.001$ ($n{=}529$--$2{,}756$), FK captures genuine inter-sample agreement rather than chance label co-occurrence.\nSplit-half reliability ranges from $r{=}0.395$ (SciERC) to $r{=}0.578$ (CoNLL LLaMA), moderate stability: FK estimates from half the samples correlate positively with those from the other half, though the SciERC value suggests noisier estimation on harder tasks with fewer samples.\nThe higher FK means on CoNLL ($0.720$--$0.927$) vs.\\ SciERC ($0.402$) mirror the difficulty gradient observed in the main quality estimation results.\n\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\\label{app:calibration}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_reliability}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Top: mean F1 vs.\\ binned confidence; bottom: instance counts per bin. Left: NER; right: RE. NER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE shows greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), RE calibration remains challenging for compound extraction tasks.}\n\\label{fig:reliability}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nReliability diagrams use 10 equal-width bins.\nNER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE exhibits greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), translating consistency scores into calibrated confidence estimates is feasible for NER but remains challenging for compound extraction tasks.\n\n\\section{Additional Analyses}\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_signal_heatmap}\n\\caption{Quality estimation heatmap (Spearman $\\rho$) across five signals and four configurations. Left: full evaluation set; right: conditional set (non-zero F1 instances). On the full set, structural signals dominate on SciERC NER while all signals converge on CoNLL. On the conditional set, EM emerges as the strongest signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$), while LP RE collapses to near-zero ($\\rho{=}0.011$).}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}\nRE signal degradation is relation-specific.\n\\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\\rho{=}0.004$), while \\textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\\rho{=}0.646$.\nThe primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives. This is a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.\nWe note that RE analysis relies on a single dataset (SciERC); generalization to other RE benchmarks remains to be validated.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability stratification.}\nFor NER, SJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.\nThe SJ advantage over LP remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}0.08$--$0.20$).\nFor RE, SJ $\\rho$ increases monotonically with sentence length (Q1: 0.110 $\\to$ Q4: 0.473; Fisher $z$-test $p{=}0.004$): longer, more relation-dense sentences provide richer structure for consistency-based estimation.\nBoth proxies are weakly correlated with F1 ($|r|{<}0.15$), they capture input complexity rather than directly predicting output quality; however, entity count and sentence length are themselves moderately correlated ($\\rho{=}0.650$), so they reflect overlapping rather than independent complexity dimensions.\nFor RE, entity count and relation count are strongly correlated ($\\rho{=}0.769$) and thus reflect a single structural complexity dimension rather than independent axes.\nOn the high-complexity RE subset ($n{=}81$, high relation count), signals improve substantially: VC reaches $\\rho{=}0.630$, LP $\\rho{=}0.554$, and SJ $\\rho{=}0.502$, all far above the full-set levels.\nRicher relational structure provides more signal for consistency-based estimation, complementing the task-level boundary established by WNUT-17: structural signals are most effective within a ``sweet spot'' of sufficient model capability and sufficient output complexity.\n\n\\paragraph{Length vs.\\ log-probability.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:length_logprob_scatter} shows that mean log-probability is weakly positively correlated with output length (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$, $n{=}4{,}232$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$, $n{=}3{,}176$), ruling out length-penalty confounding as an explanation for LP's selection failure: longer outputs receive \\textit{higher}, not lower, token-averaged log-probability under constrained decoding.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_length_logprob_scatter}\n\\caption{Output length vs.\\ mean log-probability on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Positive correlations (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$) rule out length-penalty confounding.}\n\\label{fig:length_logprob_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation circularity and LOO-SJ equivalence.}\nConsensus-proximity selection chooses $\\hat{y} = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\text{SJ}(y_i, y_j)$.\nLeave-one-out SJ (LOO-SJ) for sample $y_i$ is $\\text{LOO-SJ}_i = \\frac{1}{N-1}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\text{SJ}(y_i, y_j)$.\nSince the $\\frac{1}{N-1}$ constant is shared across all candidates within an instance, $\\arg\\max_{y_i} \\text{LOO-SJ}_i = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\text{SJ}(y_i, y_j)$; the two selection rules are equivalent.\nStandard (non-LOO) SJ adds the self-comparison term $\\text{SJ}(y_i, y_i) = 1$ to each candidate, which is constant and thus does not affect ranking.\nEmpirically, LOO-SJ yields $\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.014$ relative to standard SJ (a correlation-level difference), with a maximum per-instance score difference of $3.1{\\times}10^{-4}$ (negligible at the individual prediction level).\nEvaluation circularity is negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{SJ normalizer robustness.}\nEquation~\\ref{eq:sj} uses $\\max(|g_i|, |g_j|)$ as the denominator; an alternative is the union size $|g_i \\cup g_j|$.\nTable~\\ref{tab:sj_normalizer} compares the two normalizers across all six model--dataset combinations ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$): $|\\Delta\\rho|{<}0.01$ in every case, the choice between max and union normalization does not affect conclusions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SJ normalizer comparison (Spearman $\\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). Max: $\\max(|g_i|,|g_j|)$; Union: $|g_i \\cup g_j|$.}\n\\label{tab:sj_normalizer}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll ccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nDataset & Model & $\\rho$(max) & $\\rho$(union) & $\\Delta\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCoNLL & Qwen3-8B & .436 & .438 & $-$.002 \\\\\nCoNLL & Qwen3-4B & .422 & .421 & $+$.000 \\\\\nCoNLL & LLaMA-8B & .431 & .432 & $-$.000 \\\\\nSciERC & Qwen3-8B & .360 & .365 & $-$.005 \\\\\nSciERC & Qwen3-4B & .422 & .423 & $-$.001 \\\\\nSciERC & LLaMA-8B & .363 & .372 & $-$.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Conditional filtering details.}\nOn LLaMA conditional RE (non-zero F1 instances), SJ recovers to $\\rho{=}0.405$ while FK drops to $\\rho{=}0.259$, consistent with the NER structural advantage; on full-set RE, however, the SJ--FK difference is non-significant ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.005$, $p{=}0.53$; preliminary).\nAt $N{=}16$, the divergence persists: SJ maintains $\\rho{=}0.308$ on conditional RE while LP drops to $\\rho{=}{-}0.026$.\nEM ranks first across all four tested conditional configurations: SciERC NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$), CoNLL Qwen $N{=}16$ ($\\rho{=}0.466$), CoNLL LLaMA 3-seed ($\\rho{=}0.462$), and SciERC RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$). This holds despite full-set rankings ranging from \\#1 (CoNLL Qwen) to \\#5 (SciERC RE).\nOn the full set, EM is diluted by trivial agreement: instances where all $N$ samples produce identical (possibly incorrect) outputs receive EM${=}1$ regardless of quality, compressing the signal's dynamic range.\nOn the conditional set, where the model demonstrably produces varied outputs, exact agreement becomes the strongest quality signal.\nThe margin over SJ is largest on RE ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.171$), where compound extraction decisions amplify output variability, and smallest on CoNLL LLaMA ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.002$), where all signals converge near $\\rho{\\approx}0.46$.\n\n\\paragraph{WNUT-17 full signal analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:wnut} reports all five signals on WNUT-17 with full-set and conditional $\\rho$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation on WNUT-17 (Qwen, $N{=}16$, $T{=}1.0$, $n{=}689$). Cond: non-zero F1 instances ($n{=}334$). Full-set $\\rho$ reflects binary success prediction; conditional $\\rho$ measures quality gradation. LP's conditional $\\rho$ is highest despite negative full-set $\\rho$.}\n\\label{tab:wnut}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & AUROC & Cond.\\ $\\rho$ & Cond.\\ $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC      & .724 & .913 & .038 & .487 \\\\\nFK      & .125 & .555 & .169 & .002 \\\\\nSJ      & .089 & .517 & .288 & ${<}$1e-7 \\\\\nEM      & .050 & .487 & .333 & ${<}$1e-9 \\\\\nLP & $-$.410 & .219 & .350 & ${<}$1e-10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\nThe full-set $p$-values are: VC ${<}$1e-112, FK $p{=}0.001$, SJ $p{=}0.020$, EM $p{=}0.194$, LP ${<}$1e-28.\nVC's high full-set $\\rho{=}0.724$ is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$, $p{=}0.49$), while LP's conditional $\\rho{=}0.350$ is the highest among all signals on the non-zero subset, full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the selection analysis to five additional configurations.\nOn LLaMA SciERC, where output diversity remains substantial (18\\% identical outputs, 39\\% unique), LP achieves the highest selection F1 (0.652) at $N{=}8$ but still falls within bootstrap noise of single-sample (0.646), replicating the gap observed on Qwen SciERC (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}).\nOn LLaMA FewNERD ($n{=}32{,}565$, 3-seed mean), LP is the only signal with positive selection ($+0.36$~pp), confirming LP as the most robust selection signal across model families (Qwen FewNERD: $+1.39$~pp, Table~\\ref{tab:fewnerd}). All structural signals degrade selection (VC $-2.39$~pp, SJ $-0.52$~pp), consistent with the Qwen pattern. LLaMA FewNERD degeneracy ($23.4{\\pm}4.7$\\%, 3 seeds) is higher than Qwen ($12.7$\\%), illustrating the capability-diversity tension: LLaMA achieves stronger greedy F1 ($0.784$ vs.\\ $0.748$, $+3.6$~pp) but at the cost of higher degeneracy ($+10.7$~pp) and reduced oracle headroom ($10.0$ vs.\\ $12.4$~pp).\nOn CoNLL, 48--87\\% of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples; this reflects a ceiling effect (single-sample F1 ${\\approx}$0.91--0.92) rather than signal failure, and selection analysis on these configurations is uninformative because there is little per-sample variance for any signal to exploit.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 (macro, instance-averaged) across configurations ($T{=}1.0$, 3-seed mean). Best non-oracle signal per row in \\textbf{bold}. LP is the only signal with positive selection on both FewNERD configurations (Qwen $+1.39$~pp, Table~\\ref{tab:fewnerd}; LLaMA $+0.36$~pp). At $N{=}16$ on LLaMA CoNLL, VC achieves small but significant gains ($+0.28$~pp, $p{<}0.03$; see text below). CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}\n\\label{tab:cross_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $n$ & Greedy & Oracle & SJ & FK & EM & LP & VC \\\\\n\\midrule\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}8$ & 529 & .646 & .778 & .643 & .639 & .634 & \\textbf{.652} & .633 \\\\\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \\textbf{.649} & \\textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLLaMA/FewNERD $N{=}8$ & 32{,}565 & .784 & .884 & .779 & .779 & .774 & \\textbf{.788} & .760 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .957 & \\textbf{.899} & .893 & .898 & .897 & .895 \\\\\nLLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \\textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}\nOur $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2025scaling}.\nFor quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\\rho$ improves by $+0.047$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nStructural signals use $\\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, each additional comparison yields diminishing information.\nThis asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\\ LP $\\rho{=}0.307$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \\textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.163$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while LP does not.\nFor sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound (more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate), but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\nThe practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.\n\n\\paragraph{$N{=}16$ CoNLL selection: small but significant.}\nWhile quality estimation benefits sublinearly from $N$-scaling, selection behavior at $N{=}16$ reveals a nuanced exception on LLaMA CoNLL.\nVC achieves the largest gain over single-sample ($+0.28$~pp, $p{=}0.003$--$0.027$ across 3 seeds, all significant), with SJ, FK, and EM at $+0.22$--$0.23$~pp and LP at $+0.12$~pp (2/3 seeds significant).\nThese gains are statistically significant but practically minimal: oracle headroom at $N{=}16$ is only 2.5~pp, so VC captures approximately 10\\% of the available headroom.\nThis reinforces the primary finding: even under the most favorable conditions (high baseline performance, more candidates, and aggregated multi-seed evidence), signal-based selection recovers only a fraction of the oracle headroom available.\n\n\\section{Bootstrap CI for Signal Comparisons}\\label{app:bootstrap_signal}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci} reports bootstrap confidence intervals for individual signal $\\rho$ and pairwise $\\Delta\\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$).\nThe SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$) is clearly significant, the structural advantage reported in \\S\\ref{sec:qe_results} is robust.\nThe VC--SJ gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.019$) is not significant; these two structural signals perform comparably.\nThe SJ--EM gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.065$) is borderline significant, with the CI lower bound at $+0.003$ and upper bound at $+0.130$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for signal $\\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$). Top: individual signals. Bottom: pairwise $\\Delta\\rho$; ``Sig.'' indicates whether the 95\\% CI excludes zero.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & +0.379 & [+0.299, +0.456] \\\\\nSJ & +0.360 & [+0.277, +0.437] \\\\\nEM & +0.295 & [+0.212, +0.371] \\\\\nFK & +0.267 & [+0.176, +0.354] \\\\\nLP & +0.205 & [+0.119, +0.291] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{0.5em}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta\\rho$ & 95\\% CI & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nSJ $-$ LP & +0.155 & [+0.074, +0.234] & Yes \\\\\nVC $-$ SJ & +0.019 & [$-$0.033, +0.073] & No \\\\\nSJ $-$ EM & +0.065 & [+0.003, +0.130] & Marginal$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\smallskip\n{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Marginally significant without multiple comparison correction; would not survive Bonferroni correction for 10 pairwise comparisons ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}}{=}0.005$).}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $N{=}16$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, permutation test), the structural advantage extends to FK comparisons.\nOn SciERC NER ($n{=}529$), SJ--FK ($\\Delta\\rho{=}0.147$--$0.160$, all $p{\\leq}0.002$) and VC--FK ($\\Delta\\rho{=}0.138$--$0.188$, all $p{\\leq}0.003$) are clearly significant across all three seeds; EM--FK is seed-dependent ($\\Delta\\rho{=}0.054$--$0.093$, $p{=}0.048$--$0.15$).\nOn CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), all signals significantly outperform FK: EM $\\Delta\\rho{=}0.071$ ($p{<}0.001$), SJ $\\Delta\\rho{=}0.056$ ($p{<}0.001$), VC $\\Delta\\rho{=}0.022$ ($p{=}0.040$).\n\n\\section{Diversity and Selection Analysis}\\label{app:diversity}\n\n% fig_selection_gap promoted to main text (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap})\n\n\\paragraph{Output diversity quantification.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:diversity} summarizes output diversity across configurations.\nOn SciERC, output diversity is substantial: Qwen produces 43\\% unique outputs per instance (mean 3.5 distinct out of $N{=}8$), with per-sample F1 standard deviation of 0.144 and mean pairwise SJ of 0.703.\nLLaMA shows similar diversity (39\\% unique, F1 std 0.11).\nOn CoNLL, diversity collapses: 59\\% (Qwen) to 89\\% (LLaMA) of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples; this reflects a ceiling effect where single-sample F1 already exceeds 0.90.\nWNUT-17 shows 55\\% identical outputs, driven by the 51\\% zero-F1 rate.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Output diversity across configurations ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). ``Identical'' = fraction of instances with all $N$ outputs identical. ``Unique'' = mean fraction of distinct outputs per instance.}\n\\label{tab:diversity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Identical & Unique \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen/SciERC & 11\\% & 43\\% \\\\\nLLaMA/SciERC & 18\\% & 39\\% \\\\\nQwen/CoNLL & 57\\% & --- \\\\\nLLaMA/CoNLL & 88\\% & --- \\\\\nWNUT-17 & 55\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-improvable fraction.}\nAcross SciERC configurations, 39--42\\% of instances contain at least one sample with F1 exceeding single-sample\\footnote{Oracle-improvable and diversity-stratified analyses use $n{=}529$ (excluding instances with empty gold annotations; see \\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}); the diversity quantification in Table~\\ref{tab:diversity} uses the full test set.}: Qwen NER 42.0\\% (222/529, mean headroom $+$31.9~pp among improvable instances), Qwen RE 41.3\\% (164/397, $+$45.3~pp), LLaMA NER 39.3\\% (208/529, $+$33.7~pp).\nOn CoNLL, only 4--9\\% of instances are improvable, consistent with the ceiling effect.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity-stratified selection gap.}\nOn the 44\\% of Qwen SciERC instances with high diversity (${\\geq}4$ unique outputs among 8 samples, $n{=}232$), oracle headroom reaches $+$16.3~pp, yet all consistency-based signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) select at or below single-sample, and LP, the only per-sample signal, achieves merely $+$0.8~pp.\nLLaMA replicates this pattern: on its high-diversity subset ($n{=}186$), oracle headroom reaches $+$20.1~pp while consistency signals remain at or below single-sample and LP captures only $+$2.0~pp.\nThis rules out insufficient diversity as an explanation for the correlation-selection gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Instance degeneracy statistics.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:degeneracy} quantifies instance degeneracy across configurations. ``Constant F1'' denotes instances where all $N$ samples receive identical instance-level F1; ``Constant utility'' denotes instances where MBR (SJ) utility is identical across candidates, preventing ranking.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Instance degeneracy statistics ($T{=}1.0$). ``Constant F1'': all $N$ samples receive identical instance-level F1. ``Constant utility'': MBR (SJ) utility identical across candidates. 4B data confirms degeneracy scales with model capability.}\n\\label{tab:degeneracy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\shortstack{SciERC 8B\\\\($N{=}8$)} & \\shortstack{SciERC 4B\\\\($N{=}8$)} & \\shortstack{CoNLL 8B\\\\($N{=}8$)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nConstant F1 & 17.8\\% & 10.4\\% & 61.7\\% \\\\\nConstant utility & 13\\% & 7.0\\% & 64\\% \\\\\nOracle gap closed (non-deg.) & 23\\% & 25.2\\% & 36\\% \\\\\nWithin-inst.\\ SJ $\\rho$ (median) & 0.524 & 0.550 & 0.990 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{TOST equivalence testing.}\\label{app:tost}\nThe primary evidence against selection gains comes from effect sizes and confidence intervals: Cohen's $d{=}0.038$ for the best signal (NER LP) and bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$3.0, $+$4.2]~pp spanning zero.\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost} supplements this with two one-sided tests (TOST), testing whether each signal's selection F1 is equivalent to single-sample within a bound $\\delta$.\nThe $\\delta$ values are contextualized against oracle headroom (12--18~pp): $\\delta{=}5$~pp corresponds to forgoing 28--38\\% of the available headroom, $\\delta{=}3$~pp forgoing 17--23\\%, and $\\delta{=}2$~pp less than 15\\%.\nThese proportions use the same test data that defines the headroom, introducing circularity: $\\delta$ is anchored to the very quantity it contextualizes.\nWe address this below with externally anchored equivalence margins.\nThe breakpoint $\\delta^*$, the minimum equivalence margin at which each signal passes TOST, ranges from approximately 1.3~pp (SJ, ${\\sim}$10\\% of oracle headroom) to 3.4~pp (FK, ${\\sim}$26\\%) on NER, and from approximately 2.9~pp (LP, ${\\sim}$16\\%) to 6.5~pp (VC, ${\\sim}$36\\%) on RE.\nOn NER ($n{=}529$), at $\\delta{=}{\\pm}2$~pp, SJ and LP are confirmed equivalent to single-sample (TOST $p{<}0.05$); at $\\delta{=}{\\pm}3$~pp, four of five signals pass; at $\\delta{=}{\\pm}5$~pp, all five pass.\nOn RE ($n{=}397$, preliminary), EM and VC fail equivalence even at $\\delta{=}{\\pm}5$~pp due to systematic underperformance ($\\Delta{=}{-}3.2$~pp and $-4.5$~pp respectively).\nThe effect sizes are negligible ($d{=}0.038$) and confidence intervals span zero, with TOST confirming equivalence at practically meaningful margins.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{TOST equivalence testing: signals vs.\\ single-sample on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). \\checkmark: equivalent at $p{<}0.05$; \\texttimes: not equivalent. $\\Delta$: mean F1 difference (signal $-$ single-sample).}\n\\label{tab:tost}\n\\begin{tabular}{l cccc cccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{NER}} & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{RE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-9}\nSignal & $\\Delta$ & $\\pm$2 & $\\pm$3 & $\\pm$5 & $\\Delta$ & $\\pm$2 & $\\pm$3 & $\\pm$5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & +0.6 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & $-$0.9 & \\texttimes & \\checkmark & \\checkmark \\\\\nSJ & +0.1 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & $-$1.8 & \\texttimes & \\texttimes & \\checkmark \\\\\nFK & $-$0.5 & \\texttimes & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & $-$1.6 & \\texttimes & \\texttimes & \\checkmark \\\\\nEM & $-$0.8 & \\texttimes & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & $-$3.2 & \\texttimes & \\texttimes & \\texttimes \\\\\nVC & $-$1.0 & \\texttimes & \\texttimes & \\checkmark & $-$4.5 & \\texttimes & \\texttimes & \\texttimes \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{External equivalence margins.}\nThe oracle-headroom anchors above introduce circularity: $\\delta$ is calibrated against the very quantity it contextualizes.\nTo resolve this, we supplement with three externally grounded margins:\n(1) $\\delta_{\\mathrm{SEM}}$: the bootstrapped standard error of single-sample F1 (0.43--1.33~pp depending on configuration), which represents measurement noise;\n(2) $\\delta_{\\mathrm{practical}}{=}0.5$~pp, the threshold below which NER differences are considered negligible \\cite{ratinov2009design};\n(3) $\\delta_{\\mathrm{literature}}{=}1.0$~pp, the approximate significant-difference threshold established in NER evaluation methodology \\cite{bergkirkpatrick2012empirical}.\n\nResults under these external anchors diverge sharply by configuration.\nOn LLaMA CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), all signals pass equivalence at every $\\delta$ ($p{<}0.001$), consistent with the near-zero selection deltas ($|\\Delta|{\\leq}0.1$~pp) and tight CIs at this sample size.\nOn Qwen CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), no signal passes at any external $\\delta$: selection F1 degrades by 0.9--1.5~pp relative to single-sample, which exceeds even $\\delta_{\\mathrm{literature}}$.\nOn SciERC NER ($n{=}529$), only SJ passes at $\\delta_{\\mathrm{SEM}}{=}1.33$~pp (the widest SEM bound); all other signals and tighter margins fail.\nThese external anchors confirm that the equivalence conclusion is configuration-dependent: LLaMA CoNLL selection is robustly equivalent to single-sample under any reasonable margin, while SciERC and Qwen CoNLL selection shows detectable degradation at externally calibrated thresholds.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparison considerations.}\\label{app:multiple_comparisons}\nOur primary selection analysis involves ${\\sim}$20 comparisons (5 signals $\\times$ 3--4 configurations).\nSince all primary selection results are non-significant ($p{>}0.05$), applying Benjamini-Hochberg correction can only make these results more conservative, strengthening the ``no signal beats greedy'' conclusion.\nThe sole positive finding, the non-tied LP subset ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), survives Bonferroni correction over the 5 signal comparisons ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}}{=}0.020 < 0.05$). However, the $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ threshold is selected post-hoc from a 7-point sweep (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}); joint correction across thresholds and signals yields $p_{\\mathrm{adj}}{=}0.14$, so this finding is exploratory.\nFor quality estimation signal rankings, differences between the top signals (e.g., VC vs.\\ SJ on SciERC) should be interpreted as descriptive rather than as statistically confirmed orderings, given the limited power of 3-seed comparisons.\n\n\\paragraph{When would consistency-based selection work?}\\label{app:when_selection_works}\nOur negative results are specific to fine-tuned models with constrained decoding on structured IE. Several conditions outside our experimental scope could restore selection headroom.\n(a)~Models with poor calibration or in-context learning setups (without fine-tuning) produce more diverse outputs, widening the gap between candidates that consistency signals can exploit.\n(b)~Closed-source APIs where log-probabilities are unavailable make consistency signals the only viable selection mechanism; our structural signals (SJ, VC, FK, EM) provide quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45) even without model-internal access.\n(c)~Ensemble or mixture-of-experts settings generate structurally diverse candidates by design, reducing instance degeneracy below the levels observed in our single-model sampling.\n(d)~Tasks where the model exhibits systematic overconfidence (e.g., hallucination-prone generation) may benefit from consensus filtering, as disagreement among samples would carry more discriminative signal than in our well-calibrated fine-tuned regime.\n\n\\section{Supervised and Model-Internal Signal Analysis}\\label{app:supervised_combination}\n\n\\subsection{Supervised Signal Combination}\n\nSupervised classifiers (logistic regression, MLP, random forest) trained on all five signals (5-fold CV, 3-seed mean, SciERC, $N{=}16$, $+17.9$~pp headroom) all perform at or below greedy (F1${=}0.644$; LR: $-0.4$~pp; MLP: $-1.6$~pp; RF: $-3.1$~pp).\nAdding pairwise interaction features does not help (AUROC 0.634 vs.\\ 0.638).\nTo test whether richer representations could close the gap, we train a DeBERTa-v3-base verifier that encodes input-output pairs and predicts per-sample F1.\nDeBERTa achieves $\\rho{=}0.329{\\pm}0.087$ (5-fold CV, 3 seeds). Despite moderate quality prediction, selection utility is poor: on SciERC, DeBERTa closes $-26.5{\\pm}26.5$\\% of the oracle gap (13 of 15 runs negative; vs.\\ LP's $+4.2{\\pm}10.7$\\%); on Few-NERD, DeBERTa recovers only $+3.5$\\% (single 80/20 split; vs.\\ LP's $+16.3$\\%).\nCross-dataset transfer fails entirely ($-27$\\% to $-72$\\% gap closure).\nNeither handcrafted signal combinations nor learned text representations close the selection gap; only model-internal log-probability provides meaningful selection signal, consistent with an information asymmetry between the generating model and external observers.\n\n\\subsection{Hidden State Probes}\\label{app:hidden_probe}\n\nThe preceding negative results suggest the bottleneck is model-internal rather than a feature engineering problem.\nWe test this directly by training probes on the generating model's hidden representations.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nFor each of $N{=}8$ samples per instance, we extract the mean-pooled final-layer hidden state ($d{=}4096$) from the generating Qwen3-8B model and train a linear probe and a two-layer MLP (hidden dim${=}256$) to predict per-sample F1 (5-fold CV, 3 seeds).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Hidden state probes vs.\\ LP for sample selection (5-fold CV ${\\times}$ 3 seeds). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation with per-sample F1. Sel.\\ F1: selection F1. Gap\\%: oracle gap closure. SciERC greedy${=}0.644$, oracle${=}0.775$.}\n\\label{tab:hidden_probe}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l ccc cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Few-NERD}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\nMethod & $\\rho$ & Sel.\\ F1 & Gap\\% & $\\rho$ & Gap\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & .283 & $\\mathbf{.650}$ & $+$4.8 & .606 & 12.3 \\\\\nHidden Linear & $.179{\\pm}.019$ & $.613{\\pm}.002$ & $-$23.4 & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{---} \\\\\nHidden MLP & $\\mathbf{.388{\\pm}.013}$ & $.637{\\pm}.003$ & $-$5.1 & $\\mathbf{.629{\\pm}.007}$ & 11.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset asymmetry.}\nOn SciERC, the MLP probe achieves higher quality correlation than LP ($\\rho{=}0.388$ vs.\\ $0.283$, $+37$\\%); hidden states encode information beyond what LP captures.\nYet higher correlation does not yield better selection: MLP selection F1 ($0.637$) falls below LP's ($0.650$), with gap closure turning negative ($-5.1$\\% vs.\\ $+4.8$\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:hidden_probe}).\nThe linear probe performs worse still ($\\rho{=}0.179$, gap closure $-23.4$\\%).\nOn Few-NERD, the MLP provides no increment over LP (gap closure $11.7$\\% vs.\\ $12.3$\\%).\nA learned token-level LP aggregator (1D CNN over per-token log-probabilities) matches mean LP on Few-NERD (gap closure $56.4$\\% vs.\\ $55.8$\\%); LP's bottleneck is not token aggregation.\nThe correlation-selection gap extends to model-internal representations: in the high-capability regime (greedy F1${=}0.644$), inter-sample F1 differences are small enough that improved ranking provides no marginal selection value, consistent with the capability-diversity tension (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\section{Multi-Seed Stability}\\label{app:multiseed}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:multiseed} reports quality estimation metrics across three random seeds for both model families on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).\nOn NER, the signal ranking VC${>}$SJ${>}$EM${>}$FK${>}$LP is identical across all six model-seed combinations, with $\\sigma{\\leq}0.025$ for all signals (rankings are stable across our 3 seeds, though statistical power with $n{=}3$ is limited).\nOn RE (Qwen only, preliminary), VC remains the top signal ($\\rho{=}0.309{\\pm}0.024$), but LP rises to \\#2 ($\\rho{=}0.297{\\pm}0.022$), above FK, SJ, and EM. This reversal from the NER ranking reflects RE's higher sensitivity to per-sample scoring.\nLLaMA absolute values are systematically lower than Qwen on NER, consistent with the directionally-consistent-but-magnitude-dependent characterization in the main text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Multi-seed quality estimation on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seeds: 42, 123, 456). NER: both models; RE: Qwen only (preliminary). Signal ranking is stable across seeds ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.025$). $\\sigma$ is population standard deviation over 3 seeds.}\n\\label{tab:multiseed}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& LP & FK & SJ & VC & EM \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{NER Spearman $\\rho$ (mean $\\pm\\sigma$)}} \\\\\nQwen & .217${\\pm}$.013 & .254${\\pm}$.016 & .364${\\pm}$.018 & .386${\\pm}$.019 & .307${\\pm}$.008 \\\\\nLLaMA & .162${\\pm}$.025 & .256${\\pm}$.024 & .325${\\pm}$.021 & .384${\\pm}$.014 & .279${\\pm}$.011 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{RE Spearman $\\rho$ (mean $\\pm\\sigma$, Qwen)}} \\\\\nQwen & .297${\\pm}$.022 & .290${\\pm}$.012 & .249${\\pm}$.002 & .309${\\pm}$.024 & .135${\\pm}$.016 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{NER AUROC (mean $\\pm\\sigma$)}} \\\\\nQwen & .579${\\pm}$.008 & .600${\\pm}$.016 & .659${\\pm}$.016 & .677${\\pm}$.011 & .631${\\pm}$.013 \\\\\nLLaMA & .565${\\pm}$.008 & .593${\\pm}$.006 & .632${\\pm}$.012 & .673${\\pm}$.009 & .606${\\pm}$.005 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}",
      "numLines": 500,

    }

503	\section{Training Configuration}\label{app:training}
504	
505	Both models are fine-tuned with identical hyperparameters (Table~\ref{tab:training}) to ensure comparisons across model families reflect model differences rather than training confounds.

538	
539	\paragraph{Prompt template.}
540	For NER, we use a structured prompt:
541	\begin{quote}
542	\small
543	\texttt{System:} You are an expert in Named Entity Recognition.\\
544	\texttt{User:} Extract all named entities from the following text. Output as JSON.\\
545	Text: \texttt{\{input\_text\}}\\
546	Entity types: \texttt{\{schema\_types\}}
547	\end{quote}
548	RE follows the same structure with ``Relation Extraction'' and relation type lists substituted accordingly.
549	
550	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
551	Inference uses vLLM with XGrammar-based JSON schema enforcement \cite{dong2024xgrammar, willard2023guided}, which constrains outputs to valid entity/relation structures matching the target schema.
552	This eliminates format errors but may compress per-sample diversity by restricting the output space.\footnote{SciERC training used \texttt{enable\_thinking=True} (LLaMA Factory 0.9.4 default), injecting empty \texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks (4--5 tokens) into each training response with loss computation; CoNLL and WNUT explicitly set \texttt{enable\_thinking=False}. Constrained decoding at inference time enforces JSON-only output, suppressing thinking tokens. See \S\ref{sec:setup} and Limitations for discussion of this confound.}
553	
554	\section{Thinking Token Impact Analysis}\label{app:thinking_token}
555	
556	Qwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint was trained with empty \texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks prepended to each response (\S\ref{sec:setup}).
557	We quantify the potential impact on LP scores through three complementary arguments.
558	
559	\paragraph{LP shift magnitude.}
560	Empty thinking tokens (mean 4.5 per sample) constitute 2.1\% of tokens on SciERC.
561	The estimated LP shift is 0.001 nats per token (2.68\% of the median within-instance LP range of 0.041 nats).
562	Because all samples share identical thinking-token LP offsets, cumulative LP rankings are invariant; mean LP changes selection for ${<}1.5\%$ of instances.
563	
564	\paragraph{Constant prefix cancellation.}
565	All $N$ samples within an instance share the same prompt and, under constrained decoding, the same absence of thinking tokens at inference time.
566	Any LP bias from the training-time thinking prefix is therefore a near-constant additive shift across all candidates within an instance, leaving within-instance LP \textit{rankings} unchanged.
567	
568	\paragraph{CoNLL control group.}
569	CoNLL and WNUT-17 checkpoints were trained without thinking tokens (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}).
570	If thinking tokens caused LP compression on SciERC, CoNLL should exhibit lower tied fractions.
571	Instead, CoNLL shows \textit{higher} tied fractions at both temperatures (86.8\% at $T{=}1.0$, 95.8\% at $T{=}0.7$) than SciERC (60.3\% at $T{=}1.0$), directly contradicting the thinking-token hypothesis.
572	
573	\section{Log-Probability Score Compression}\label{app:logprob_ties}
574	
575	Under constrained decoding, log-probability scores are severely compressed within instances, limiting per-sample discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:logprob_ties}, Figure~\ref{fig:lp_range_cdf}).
576	The median within-instance LP range is only 0.041 nats; we use $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ as an illustrative threshold to characterize this compression.
577	At this cutpoint, 60.3\% of instances ($n{=}529$, excluding empty gold annotations) have a within-sample range below $\varepsilon$, and 75\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\Delta|{<}0.026$).
578	The CDF rises smoothly with no cliff or discontinuity (Figure~\ref{fig:lp_range_cdf}); compression is a pervasive regime rather than a bimodal split.
579	This compression is stable across seeds: seed${=}456$ yields median LP range${=}0.040$ nats and tied fraction${=}62.2\%$ (vs.\ seed${=}42$: $0.041$ nats, $60.3\%$).
580	Seed~456 uses a different subtask flag (joint vs.\ ner for seeds 42 and 123); the LP distributions are statistically indistinguishable (median LP: 8.37 vs.\ 8.20 nats; Mann--Whitney $p{=}0.55$); the subtask change does not affect compression.
581	For comparison, SJ has only 12.5\% tied instances at the same threshold; SJ's advantage over LP for selection is one of score resolution, not information content.

607	\paragraph{Non-tied subset.}
608	Among instances with LP range ${\geq}0.05$ ($n{=}210$, 40\% of total), log-probability selection \textit{significantly} outperforms single-sample ($+2.9$~pp F1, $p{=}0.004$), and within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$ rises from 0.08 (tied subset) to 0.39 (non-tied subset).
609	As a continuous complement to the binary tied/non-tied analysis, pooled within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1}){=}0.248$ across all instances confirms weak but positive per-sample signal; however, only 21.6\% of instances achieve $p{<}0.05$ significance, and $\rho(\text{LP range}, \text{F1 range}){=}0.328$ shows that instances with more LP spread also exhibit more F1 variability. LP carries information, but compression limits its exploitation.
610	A free-form NER decoding ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}) confirms this compression persists without constrained decoding.

615	$\varepsilon{=}0.05$ yields the strongest statistical separation ($p{=}0.004$); at $\varepsilon{\geq}0.15$, the non-tied subset becomes too small for reliable inference ($n{\leq}36$).

734	The NER pattern reveals two complementary failure modes rather than a monotonic relationship between consistency and selection gain:
735	\begin{itemize}
736	\item \textbf{Q4 ceiling effect.} High-consistency instances (mean VC${=}0.92$) have oracle headroom of only 4.78~pp. Samples have already converged, leaving little room for any signal to improve selection.
737	\item \textbf{Q1--Q2 noise.} Low-consistency instances retain large headroom (14--17~pp) but LP rankings are unreliable, and LP rankings produce inconsistent $\Delta$ signs across quartiles.
738	\end{itemize}
739	
740	On $^\dagger$RE, the pattern differs: Q4 LP achieves $+4.01$~pp; LP may recover selection ability when high consistency coincides with structured output diversity. This is a single-dataset observation and should be interpreted cautiously.
741	
742	On CoNLL NER, Q3--Q4 instances have VC${=}1.0$ (all samples identical), and $\Delta{=}0$ universally; this ceiling effect is even more extreme than SciERC Q4.

744	\section{Exploratory Extension to Relation Extraction}\label{app:re_extension}
745	
746	We extend the NER evaluation to relation extraction (RE) on SciERC as an exploratory single-benchmark analysis.
747	RE produces sets of (head, relation, tail) triples with partial overlap; 40\% of RE instances receive zero F1, and entity span errors account for 55.5\% of RE false negatives, a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect (\S\ref{app:additional}).
748	All RE results use Qwen3-8B unless noted; single-seed ($\text{seed}{=}42$) for $N{=}8$ analyses, 3-seed for $N{=}16$.
749	
750	\paragraph{Quality estimation.}
751	RE quality estimation is reported in Table~\ref{tab:multiseed} (3-seed means).
752	The signal ranking differs from NER: VC leads ($\rho{=}0.309{\pm}0.024$), but LP rises to \#2 ($\rho{=}0.297{\pm}0.022$), above FK, SJ, and EM, a reversal reflecting RE's higher sensitivity to per-sample scoring.
753	RE miscalibration is more severe than NER (ECE 0.109--0.339; Figure~\ref{fig:reliability}).
754	
755	\paragraph{Selection.}
756	Table~\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} presents RE selection at $N{=}8$: no signal outperforms single-sample (0.404) on the full set ($n{=}397$) or the non-zero subset ($n{=}238$), with 18.1~pp of unrealized oracle headroom.
757	At $N{=}16$ (Qwen3-4B, Table~\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}), LP is the sole positive signal ($\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$), consistent with LP being the strongest RE selection signal across scales.
758	
759	\paragraph{Scale ablation.}
760	At 4B scale (Qwen3-4B, $N{=}8$, $\text{seed}{=}42$), RE quality estimation shows FK $\rho{=}0.359$ and LP $\rho{=}0.351$, both above their 8B counterparts (FK 0.275, LP 0.266), while SJ declines ($0.267$ vs.\ $0.250$).
761	Selection remains below single-sample on RE ($\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 17.1~pp of oracle headroom.
762	
763	\paragraph{Free-form decoding.}
764	RE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\pm}0.1$~pp across 4 seeds vs.\ $-0.9$~pp under constrained decoding at 8B; \S\ref{app:freeform_ablation}); constrained decoding's fixed template likely obscures LP signal in RE (\S\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).
765	
766	\subsection{RE Conditional Selection}\label{app:re_conditional_selection}
767	
768	Table~\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} extends the selection analysis to the RE non-zero F1 subset.
769	Of $n{=}397$ RE instances, 238 (60\%) have non-zero F1 and 159 (40\%) have zero F1.
770	All five signals underperform single-sample on both subsets.
771	On the non-zero subset, selection performance is \textit{worse} than on the full set (e.g., VC: $-10.0$~pp on non-zero vs.\ $-4.5$~pp on full set), because single-sample is already strong on non-zero instances (F1${=}0.674$) and signals actively select inferior samples.
772	Oracle headroom exists (0.757 vs.\ 0.674) but no signal exploits it, ruling out the 40\% zero-F1 floor effect as the explanation for RE selection failure.

826	\section{MBR Decoding and Task-Specific Interactions}\label{app:mbr_discussion}
827	
828	\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}
829	Our selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\hat{y} = \arg\max_{y_i} \sum_{j \neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.
830	Despite MBR's success in machine translation \cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here: despite positive within-instance utility--F1 correlation, selection gains are limited by instance degeneracy, the core of the correlation-selection gap.
831	\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs, consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.
832	
833	\paragraph{Task-specific decoding interactions.}
834	The free-form ablation reveals divergent behavior across tasks.
835	On RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).
836	The win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).
837	This interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.
838	This ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); whether the RE reversal replicates across seeds and generalizes to other RE benchmarks warrants further investigation.
839	The non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472: 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, and the newly non-tied subset shows a $3{\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.
840	Despite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible. Score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.
841	Free-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\rho$: 0.379$\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.
842	
843	\section{Free-Form Decoding Ablation}\label{app:freeform_ablation}
844	
845	Table~\ref{tab:freeform_ablation} compares constrained and free-form decoding across four dimensions on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B; seed${=}42$ shown, 4-seed RE statistics below).
846	Free-form decoding removes grammar constraints entirely; outputs are parsed post-hoc (parse rate 99.95\%, 4406/4408).
847	LP score distributions are nearly identical across regimes (Panel~A: within-instance std ratio $0.97{\times}$), and NER selection gains remain negligible under both regimes (Panel~B).
848	RE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\pm}0.12$~pp (seeds 42/123/456/789: $+1.69$, $+1.76$, $+2.00$, $+1.91$~pp) vs.\ $-0.68$~pp constrained.
849	The constrained reference ($-0.68$~pp) uses the ablation-matched baseline; the primary experiment (Table~\ref{tab:selection}) yields $-0.9$~pp on the same gold-filtered set.
850	Quality estimation improves modestly for most signals under free-form decoding (Panel~C), with the largest gain for FK ($+0.053$ NER, $+0.039$ RE).

927	
928	\paragraph{CoNLL NER free-form results.}
929	Extending the free-form ablation to CoNLL NER (Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), LP range median is $0.058{\pm}0.007$ across seeds, higher than SciERC ($0.041$) but still compressed given CoNLL's 86.8\% tied fraction under constrained decoding.
930	Free-form decoding reduces the tied fraction modestly, yet selection remains below single-sample: LP-selected F1${=}0.896$ vs.\ single-sample $0.904$ ($\Delta{=}{-}0.77$~pp).
931	Quality estimation under free-form decoding yields SJ $\rho{=}0.461$, EM $\rho{=}0.471$, LP $\rho{=}0.326$, comparable to constrained results (Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).
932	The failure to improve selection despite reduced compression is consistent with CoNLL's low oracle headroom (5.0~pp at $N{=}8$), which limits the selection space regardless of score distribution quality.
933	
934	\section{LP Selection Failure Stability}\label{app:lp_failure_stability}
935	
936	We analyze RE LP selection failures across three random seeds (constrained decoding, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B) to assess whether failures are systematic or stochastic.
937	
938	\paragraph{Pairwise overlap.}
939	LP selection failures (instances where LP-selected F1 $<$ single-sample F1) show moderate cross-seed stability: the average pairwise Jaccard similarity is 0.42 (range: 0.38--0.46); failures are partially systematic (driven by instance complexity) but not fully deterministic.
940	The 3-way intersection contains 34 instances (27.9\% of any-seed failures), which constitutes a stable failure core.
941	
942	\paragraph{Instance complexity drives failure.}
943	Table~\ref{tab:stable_failures} stratifies instances by failure stability.
944	Stable failures (all 3 seeds) involve more gold relations (2.12 vs.\ 1.62 for never-fail instances) and higher single-sample F1 (0.688 vs.\ 0.429); LP mis-selects on instances where the model already performs well, precisely the instances where selection would be most valuable.
945	Failure rate increases monotonically with gold relation count: 4.3\% at 1 relation, 20.7\% at 3 relations, 26.2\% at 6 relations.
946

948	\centering
949	\small
950	\caption{RE LP selection failure stability across 3 seeds (SciERC, $N{=}8$). Stable failures concentrate on relation-dense instances with high single-sample F1.}
951	\label{tab:stable_failures}
952	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
953	\toprule
954	Group & $n$ & Gold rels & Greedy F1 & LP $\Delta$F1 \\
955	\midrule
956	Stable fail (3 seeds) & 34 & 2.12 & 0.688 & $-$0.499 \\
957	Fail 2+ seeds & 26 & 2.46 & 0.774 & $-$0.450 \\
958	Fail 1 seed & 62 & 2.32 & 0.692 & $-$0.239 \\
959	Never fail & 429 & 1.62 & 0.429 & $+$0.041 \\
960	\bottomrule
961	\end{tabular}
962	\end{table}
963	
964	\paragraph{Flip instances.}
965	Only 5 RE instances with gold relations flip from LP failure (constrained) to LP success (free-form), with mean gold relation count 3.67 (well above the population mean of 1.86).
966	These instances show large constrained LP errors ($\Delta{=}{-}0.430$) that reverse under free-form decoding ($\Delta{=}{+}0.503$), but their rarity (5/397) confirms that the RE selection flip is driven by aggregate variance reallocation rather than systematic instance-level correction.
967

Output as JSON.\\\\\nText: \\texttt{\\{input\\_text\\}}\\\\\nEntity types: \\texttt{\\{schema\\_types\\}}\n\\end{quote}\nRE follows the same structure with ``Relation Extraction'' and relation type lists substituted accordingly.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nInference uses vLLM with XGrammar-based JSON schema enforcement \\cite{dong2024xgrammar, willard2023guided}, which constrains outputs to valid entity/relation structures matching the target schema.\nThis eliminates format errors but may compress per-sample diversity by restricting the output space.\\footnote{SciERC training used \\texttt{enable\\_thinking=True} (LLaMA Factory 0.9.4 default), injecting empty \\texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks (4--5 tokens) into each training response with loss computation; CoNLL and WNUT explicitly set \\texttt{enable\\_thinking=False}. Constrained decoding at inference time enforces JSON-only output, suppressing thinking tokens. See \\S\\ref{sec:setup} and Limitations for discussion of this confound.}\n\n\\section{Thinking Token Impact Analysis}\\label{app:thinking_token}\n\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint was trained with empty \\texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks prepended to each response (\\S\\ref{sec:setup}).\nWe quantify the potential impact on LP scores through three complementary arguments.\n\n\\paragraph{LP shift magnitude.}\nEmpty thinking tokens (mean 4.5 per sample) constitute 2.1\\% of tokens on SciERC.\nThe estimated LP shift is 0.001 nats per token (2.68\\% of the median within-instance LP range of 0.041 nats).\nBecause all samples share identical thinking-token LP offsets, cumulative LP rankings are invariant; mean LP changes selection for ${<}1.5\\%$ of instances.\n\n\\paragraph{Constant prefix cancellation.}\nAll $N$ samples within an instance share the same prompt and, under constrained decoding, the same absence of thinking tokens at inference time.\nAny LP bias from the training-time thinking prefix is therefore a near-constant additive shift across all candidates within an instance, leaving within-instance LP \\textit{rankings} unchanged.\n\n\\paragraph{CoNLL control group.}\nCoNLL and WNUT-17 checkpoints were trained without thinking tokens (\\texttt{enable\\_thinking=False}).\nIf thinking tokens caused LP compression on SciERC, CoNLL should exhibit lower tied fractions.\nInstead, CoNLL shows \\textit{higher} tied fractions at both temperatures (86.8\\% at $T{=}1.0$, 95.8\\% at $T{=}0.7$) than SciERC (60.3\\% at $T{=}1.0$), directly contradicting the thinking-token hypothesis.\n\n\\section{Log-Probability Score Compression}\\label{app:logprob_ties}\n\nUnder constrained decoding, log-probability scores are severely compressed within instances, limiting per-sample discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:logprob_ties}, Figure~\\ref{fig:lp_range_cdf}).\nThe median within-instance LP range is only 0.041 nats; we use $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ as an illustrative threshold to characterize this compression.\nAt this cutpoint, 60.3\\% of instances ($n{=}529$, excluding empty gold annotations) have a within-sample range below $\\varepsilon$, and 75\\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\\Delta|{<}0.026$).\nThe CDF rises smoothly with no cliff or discontinuity (Figure~\\ref{fig:lp_range_cdf}); compression is a pervasive regime rather than a bimodal split.\nThis compression is stable across seeds: seed${=}456$ yields median LP range${=}0.040$ nats and tied fraction${=}62.2\\%$ (vs.\\ seed${=}42$: $0.041$ nats, $60.3\\%$).\nSeed~456 uses a different subtask flag (joint vs.\\ ner for seeds 42 and 123); the LP distributions are statistically indistinguishable (median LP: 8.37 vs.\\ 8.20 nats; Mann--Whitney $p{=}0.55$); the subtask change does not affect compression.\nFor comparison, SJ has only 12.5\\% tied instances at the same threshold; SJ's advantage over LP for selection is one of score resolution, not information content.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_lp_range_cdf}\n\\caption{CDF of within-instance LP range on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$). The median LP range is 0.041 nats (dashed line), below $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$. The smooth CDF confirms compression is pervasive, not bimodal.}\n\\label{fig:lp_range_cdf}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Log-probability score compression on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$). ``Tied'' = fraction of instances where within-instance LP range ${<}\\varepsilon$.}\n\\label{tab:logprob_ties}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n$\\varepsilon$ threshold & Tied instances (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.01 & 1.1\\% \\\\\n0.05 & 60.3\\% \\\\\n0.1  & ${\\sim}$84\\% \\\\\n0.5  & ${\\sim}$99\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-tied subset.}\nAmong instances with LP range ${\\geq}0.05$ ($n{=}210$, 40\\% of total), log-probability selection \\textit{significantly} outperforms single-sample ($+2.9$~pp F1, $p{=}0.004$), and within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$ rises from 0.08 (tied subset) to 0.39 (non-tied subset).\nAs a continuous complement to the binary tied/non-tied analysis, pooled within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1}){=}0.248$ across all instances confirms weak but positive per-sample signal; however, only 21.6\\% of instances achieve $p{<}0.05$ significance, and $\\rho(\\text{LP range}, \\text{F1 range}){=}0.328$ shows that instances with more LP spread also exhibit more F1 variability. LP carries information, but compression limits its exploitation.\nA free-form NER decoding ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}) confirms this compression persists without constrained decoding.\n\n\\paragraph{$\\varepsilon$ sensitivity sweep (post-hoc exploratory).}\nTable~\\ref{tab:epsilon_sweep} sweeps the tied/non-tied threshold from $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$ to $0.20$ as a post-hoc exploratory analysis characterizing how selection gain varies with compression severity.\nThe tied fraction increases smoothly with no cliff or discontinuity, and the selection gain $\\Delta$ grows monotonically as the non-tied subset narrows to progressively higher-spread instances.\n$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ yields the strongest statistical separation ($p{=}0.004$); at $\\varepsilon{\\geq}0.15$, the non-tied subset becomes too small for reliable inference ($n{\\leq}36$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{$\\varepsilon$ sensitivity sweep for LP selection on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, $n{=}529$). ``Tied\\%'' = fraction with within-instance LP range ${<}\\varepsilon$. $\\Delta$: selection F1 gain over single-sample on the non-tied subset. The tied fraction increases smoothly; $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ yields the strongest statistical separation.}\n\\label{tab:epsilon_sweep}\n\\begin{tabular}{ccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\varepsilon$ & Tied\\% & Non-tied $n$ & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.01 & 1.1\\% & 523 & $+$0.58 & .21 \\\\\n0.02 & 13.6\\% & 457 & $+$0.83 & .15 \\\\\n0.03 & 31.8\\% & 361 & $+$1.31 & .079 \\\\\n\\textbf{0.05} & \\textbf{60.3\\%} & \\textbf{210} & \\textbf{$+$2.86} & \\textbf{.004} \\\\\n0.10 & 83.9\\% & 85 & $+$3.69 & .034 \\\\\n0.15 & 93.2\\% & 36 & $+$4.15 & .11 \\\\\n0.20 & 96.4\\% & 19 & $+$4.59 & .13 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{$\\varepsilon$ robustness of selection F1.}\\label{app:epsilon_robustness}\nThe preceding analysis stratifies LP by a tied/non-tied threshold; consistency-based signals (SJ, FK, VC, EM) also use $\\varepsilon$ to define MBR-style proximity in selection.\nTable~\\ref{tab:epsilon_robustness} reports the maximum selection F1 variation across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ for all signals and configurations.\nAt $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$, consistency signals change by at most 1.09~pp (mean 0.21~pp), with EM completely $\\varepsilon$-invariant (integer scoring); LP is the most sensitive (up to 3.65~pp across the full range), as expected.\nSpearman $\\rho$, the primary quality estimation metric throughout, is a continuous rank correlation that does not depend on any discretization threshold; the $\\varepsilon$ parameter affects only the selection analysis.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Maximum selection F1 variation (pp) across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$.
