Full $\\varepsilon$ sweep in Table~\\ref{tab:epsilon_sweep}.}\n\\label{tab:nontied_main}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nSubset & $n$ & $\\rho$(LP,F1) & LP $\\Delta$ vs.\\ greedy \\\\\n\\midrule\nTied (range ${<}0.05$) & 319 & 0.08 & ${\\approx}0$ \\\\\nNon-tied (range ${\\geq}0.05$) & 210 & 0.39 & $+$2.9~pp ($p{=}0.004$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nAll instances & 529 & 0.248 & $+$0.2~pp ($p{=}0.29$) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Within-instance LP-quality alignment.}\\label{app:within_instance_rho}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:within_rho} reports median within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$ across datasets, computed over non-degenerate instances (those with ${\\geq}2$ distinct F1 values across $N{=}8$ samples).\nFew-NERD exhibits substantially stronger alignment ($\\rho{=}0.667$) than SciERC ($\\rho{=}0.262$), directly explaining why LP selection succeeds only on Few-NERD.\nCoNLL alignment is moderate ($\\rho{\\approx}0.51$), but the valid-instance fraction is low (38\\% for Qwen, 11\\% for LLaMA) because degeneracy eliminates most headroom before alignment can operate.\nLP-quality alignment is the primary predictor of selection direction; degeneracy constrains headroom but does not independently predict whether LP selection gains materialize (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-instance LP-quality alignment across datasets ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). $\\rho$: median Spearman correlation between LP and F1 within each instance, over non-degenerate instances ($n_{\\text{valid}}$: ${\\geq}2$ distinct F1 values). Counts are from the primary seed (seed 42); text references use 3-seed mean degeneracy rates.}\n\\label{tab:within_rho}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nDataset & Model & Med.\\ $\\rho$ & $n_{\\text{valid}}$ / $n_{\\text{total}}$ & Valid\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nSciERC & Qwen3-8B & 0.262 & 435 / 529 & 82\\% \\\\\nFew-NERD & Qwen3-8B & 0.667 & 28{,}055 / 32{,}565 & 86\\% \\\\\nCoNLL & Qwen3-8B & 0.506 & 1{,}124 / 2{,}756 & 41\\% \\\\\nCoNLL & LLaMA-3.1-8B & 0.512 & 298 / 2{,}756 & 11\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Consistency-Stratified Selection Analysis}\\label{app:vc_quartile}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:vc_quartile} stratifies SciERC instances by voting confidence (VC) quartiles and reports selection $\\Delta$ (gain over single-sample) for LP and SJ.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Selection $\\Delta$ (pp over single-sample) stratified by VC quartile on SciERC ($N{=}8$, Qwen3-8B). Oracle headroom collapses at Q4; LP rankings are unreliable at Q1--Q2. Neither quartile supports consistent selection gains.}\n\\label{tab:vc_quartile}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nQuartile & Mean VC & LP $\\Delta$ & SJ $\\Delta$ & Oracle (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\textit{NER}} \\\\\nQ1 (low) & 0.30 & $+$1.55 & $+$0.54 & 16.85 \\\\\nQ2 & 0.51 & $-$0.65 & $-$1.89 & 14.35 \\\\\nQ3 & 0.68 & $+$1.97 & $+$1.84 & 16.83 \\\\\nQ4 (high) & 0.92 & $-$0.41 & $-$0.04 & 4.78 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\textit{$^\\dagger$RE}} \\\\\nQ1 (low) & --- & $-$3.56 & --- & --- \\\\\nQ4 (high) & --- & $+$4.01 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe NER pattern reveals two complementary failure modes rather than a monotonic relationship between consistency and selection gain:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Q4 ceiling effect.} High-consistency instances (mean VC${=}0.92$) have oracle headroom of only 4.78~pp. Samples have already converged, leaving little room for any signal to improve selection.\n\\item \\textbf{Q1--Q2 noise.} Low-consistency instances retain large headroom (14--17~pp) but LP rankings are unreliable, and LP rankings produce inconsistent $\\Delta$ signs across quartiles.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nOn $^\\dagger$RE, the pattern differs: Q4 LP achieves $+4.01$~pp; LP may recover selection ability when high consistency coincides with structured output diversity. This is a single-dataset observation and should be interpreted cautiously.\n\nOn CoNLL NER, Q3--Q4 instances have VC${=}1.0$ (all samples identical), and $\\Delta{=}0$ universally; this ceiling effect is even more extreme than SciERC Q4.\n\n\\section{Exploratory Extension to Relation Extraction}\\label{app:re_extension}\n\nWe extend the NER evaluation to relation extraction (RE) on SciERC as an exploratory single-benchmark analysis.\nRE produces sets of (head, relation, tail) triples with partial overlap; 40\\% of RE instances receive zero F1, and entity span errors account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives, a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect (\\S\\ref{app:additional}).\nAll RE results use Qwen3-8B unless noted; single-seed ($\\text{seed}{=}42$) for $N{=}8$ analyses, 3-seed for $N{=}16$.\n\n\\paragraph{Quality estimation.}\nRE quality estimation is reported in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed} (3-seed means).\nThe signal ranking differs from NER: VC leads ($\\rho{=}0.309{\\pm}0.024$), but LP rises to \\#2 ($\\rho{=}0.297{\\pm}0.022$), above FK, SJ, and EM, a reversal reflecting RE's higher sensitivity to per-sample scoring.\nRE miscalibration is more severe than NER (ECE 0.109--0.339; Figure~\\ref{fig:reliability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Selection.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} presents RE selection at $N{=}8$: no signal outperforms single-sample (0.404) on the full set ($n{=}397$) or the non-zero subset ($n{=}238$), with 18.1~pp of unrealized oracle headroom.\nAt $N{=}16$ (Qwen3-4B, Table~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}), LP is the sole positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$), consistent with LP being the strongest RE selection signal across scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale ablation.}\nAt 4B scale (Qwen3-4B, $N{=}8$, $\\text{seed}{=}42$), RE quality estimation shows FK $\\rho{=}0.359$ and LP $\\rho{=}0.351$, both above their 8B counterparts (FK 0.275, LP 0.266), while SJ declines ($0.267$ vs.\\ $0.250$).\nSelection remains below single-sample on RE ($\\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 17.1~pp of oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{Free-form decoding.}\nRE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\\pm}0.1$~pp across 4 seeds vs.\\ $-0.9$~pp under constrained decoding at 8B; \\S\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}); constrained decoding's fixed template likely obscures LP signal in RE (\\S\\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).\n\n\\subsection{RE Conditional Selection}\\label{app:re_conditional_selection}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} extends the selection analysis to the RE non-zero F1 subset.\nOf $n{=}397$ RE instances, 238 (60\\%) have non-zero F1 and 159 (40\\%) have zero F1.\nAll five signals underperform single-sample on both subsets.\nOn the non-zero subset, selection performance is \\textit{worse} than on the full set (e.g., VC: $-10.0$~pp on non-zero vs.\\ $-4.5$~pp on full set), because single-sample is already strong on non-zero instances (F1${=}0.674$) and signals actively select inferior samples.\nOracle headroom exists (0.757 vs.\\ 0.674) but no signal exploits it, ruling out the 40\\% zero-F1 floor effect as the explanation for RE selection failure.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{RE selection F1 (macro, instance-averaged) on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Full set ($n{=}397$) and non-zero F1 subset ($n{=}238$). No signal outperforms greedy on either subset.}\n\\label{tab:re_cond_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Full ($n{=}397$) & Non-zero ($n{=}238$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & 0.585 & 0.757 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & 0.395 & 0.600 \\\\\nSJ & 0.386 & 0.604 \\\\\nEM & 0.372 & 0.583 \\\\\nVC & 0.359 & 0.574 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy & 0.404 & 0.674 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{RE $N{=}16$ Selection Analysis}\\label{app:re_n16_selection}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection} extends the selection analysis to RE at $N{=}16$ with 3-seed statistics.\nAll four consistency signals underperform single-sample, with EM showing the largest deficit ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.5$~pp, Fisher $p{>}0.99$).\nLP is the only positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, Fisher $p{=}0.058$), driven primarily by seed~456 ($\\Delta{=}{+}1.5$~pp, $p{=}0.026$); the remaining seeds show smaller effects ($+0.1$ and $+0.6$~pp, $p{>}0.20$).\nEven the most favorable seed captures only ${\\sim}9\\%$ of the 17.1~pp oracle headroom.\nThis cross-task consistency (LP as the only viable selection signal on both NER and RE) reinforces the persistent limitation of instance-level signals for candidate ranking across tested regimes.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{RE best-of-$N$ selection F1 (macro, instance-averaged) on SciERC (Qwen3-4B, $N{=}16$, $T{=}1.0$, 3-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, $n{=}551$). Fisher combined $p$ vs.\\ greedy. LP is the only signal with positive $\\Delta$. $\\sigma$ is population standard deviation over 3 seeds.}\n\\label{tab:re_n16_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & F1 & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$(Fisher) \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $.463{\\pm}.006$ & $+$17.1 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & $\\mathbf{.299}{\\pm}.007$ & $+$0.7 & .058 \\\\\nVC & $.284{\\pm}.002$ & $-$0.8 & .973 \\\\\nFK & $.283{\\pm}.005$ & $-$0.9 & .985 \\\\\nSJ & $.281{\\pm}.005$ & $-$1.0 & .993 \\\\\nEM & $.277{\\pm}.007$ & $-$1.5 & .999 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy & $.291{\\pm}.000$ & --- & --- \\\\\nRandom & $.262{\\pm}.001$ & $-$2.9 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{MBR Decoding and Task-Specific Interactions}\\label{app:mbr_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}\nOur selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\\hat{y} = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.\nDespite MBR's success in machine translation \\cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \\cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here: despite positive within-instance utility--F1 correlation, selection gains are limited by instance degeneracy, the core of the correlation-selection gap.\n\\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs, consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.\n\n\\paragraph{Task-specific decoding interactions.}\nThe free-form ablation reveals divergent behavior across tasks.\nOn RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).\nThe win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).\nThis interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.\nThis ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); whether the RE reversal replicates across seeds and generalizes to other RE benchmarks warrants further investigation.\nThe non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472: 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, and the newly non-tied subset shows a $3{\\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.\nDespite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible. Score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.\nFree-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\\rho$: 0.379$\\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.\n\n\\section{Free-Form Decoding Ablation}\\label{app:freeform_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation} compares constrained and free-form decoding across four dimensions on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B; seed${=}42$ shown, 4-seed RE statistics below).\nFree-form decoding removes grammar constraints entirely; outputs are parsed post-hoc (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408).\nLP score distributions are nearly identical across regimes (Panel~A: within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$), and NER selection gains remain negligible under both regimes (Panel~B).\nRE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\\pm}0.12$~pp (seeds 42/123/456/789: $+1.69$, $+1.76$, $+2.00$, $+1.91$~pp) vs.\\ $-0.68$~pp constrained.\nThe constrained reference ($-0.68$~pp) uses the ablation-matched baseline; the primary experiment (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}) yields $-0.9$~pp on the same gold-filtered set.\nQuality estimation improves modestly for most signals under free-form decoding (Panel~C), with the largest gain for FK ($+0.053$ NER, $+0.039$ RE).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of constrained vs.\\ free-form decoding on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$; RE selection gap replicates across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\\pm}0.12$~pp).}\n\\label{tab:freeform_ablation}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel A: Logprob Distribution}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & Constrained & Free-form & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean LP std        & 0.0206 & 0.0199 & $-$0.0007 \\\\\nMean LP range      & 0.0623 & 0.0599 & $-$0.0024 \\\\\nTied fraction ($\\varepsilon{=}0.05$) & 59.7\\% & 57.7\\% & $-$2.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{8pt}\n\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel B: Selection F1}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\nTask & Method & Constrained & Free-form & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{NER}\n  & Greedy        & 0.6182 & 0.6204 & $+$0.0022 \\\\\n  & LP-selected   & 0.6241 & 0.6222 & $-$0.0019 \\\\\n  & Selection gap & $+$0.0059 & $+$0.0018 & $-$0.0041 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{RE}\n  & Greedy        & 0.2913 & 0.2880 & $-$0.0033 \\\\\n  & LP-selected   & 0.2845 & 0.3050 & \\textbf{$+$0.0205} \\\\\n  & Selection gap & $-$0.0068 & $+$0.0169 & \\textbf{$+$0.0237} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{8pt}\n\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel C: QE Signal Quality (Spearman $\\rho$)}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n & \\\\\nEM & 0.295 & 0.317 & $+$0.023 & 0.134 & 0.137 & $+$0.002 \\\\\nVC & 0.379 & 0.409 & $+$0.030 & 0.350 & 0.323 & $-$0.026 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{8pt}\n\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel D: Parse Rate (Free-form)}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal samples          & 4408 \\\\\nParse rate             & 99.95\\% \\\\\nInstances 8/8 valid    & 549\\,/\\,551 (99.6\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{CoNLL NER free-form results.}\nExtending the free-form ablation to CoNLL NER (Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), LP range median is $0.058{\\pm}0.007$ across seeds, higher than SciERC ($0.041$) but still compressed given CoNLL's 86.8\\% tied fraction under constrained decoding.\nFree-form decoding reduces the tied fraction modestly, yet selection remains below single-sample: LP-selected F1${=}0.896$ vs.\\ single-sample $0.904$ ($\\Delta{=}{-}0.77$~pp).\nQuality estimation under free-form decoding yields SJ $\\rho{=}0.461$, EM $\\rho{=}0.471$, LP $\\rho{=}0.326$, comparable to constrained results (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).\nThe failure to improve selection despite reduced compression is consistent with CoNLL's low oracle headroom (5.0~pp at $N{=}8$), which limits the selection space regardless of score distribution quality.\n\n\\section{LP Selection Failure Stability}\\label{app:lp_failure_stability}\n\nWe analyze RE LP selection failures across three random seeds (constrained decoding, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B) to assess whether failures are systematic or stochastic.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise overlap.}\nLP selection failures (instances where LP-selected F1 $<$ single-sample F1) show moderate cross-seed stability: the average pairwise Jaccard similarity is 0.42 (range: 0.38--0.46); failures are partially systematic (driven by instance complexity) but not fully deterministic.\nThe 3-way intersection contains 34 instances (27.9\\% of any-seed failures), which constitutes a stable failure core.\n\n\\paragraph{Instance complexity drives failure.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:stable_failures} stratifies instances by failure stability.\nStable failures (all 3 seeds) involve more gold relations (2.12 vs.\\ 1.62 for never-fail instances) and higher single-sample F1 (0.688 vs.\\ 0.429); LP mis-selects on instances where the model already performs well, precisely the instances where selection would be most valuable.\nFailure rate increases monotonically with gold relation count: 4.3\\% at 1 relation, 20.7\\% at 3 relations, 26.2\\% at 6 relations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{RE LP selection failure stability 429 & 1.62 & 0.429 & $+$0.041 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Flip instances.}\nOnly 5 RE instances with gold relations flip from LP failure (constrained) to LP success This illustrates that low degeneracy does not ensure selection gains; LP-quality alignment determines selection direction.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1411:At 10 epochs, the model retains competitive greedy F1 (0.649 vs.\\ 0.650 at 3 epochs) yet conditions are unfavorable: 47.8\\% degenerate instances and $\\rho{=}0.112$.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1412:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1418:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1425:Instance degen.\\ (\\%) & 6.4 & 17.8 & 47.8 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1426:LP $\\Delta$ (pp) & $-$1.24 & $+$0.64 & $+$1.02 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1435:The Few-NERD epoch analysis covers 2 points (3 vs.\\ 5 epochs); 1037	\paragraph{$N$-scaling: selection headroom vs.\ selection gain.}
1038	Table~\ref{tab:n_scaling_selection} traces the effect of sample count on degeneracy, oracle headroom, and per-signal selection gain.
1039	Oracle headroom grows roughly linearly with $N$ ($+4.4$~pp at $N{=}2$ to $+20.0$~pp at $N{=}32$).
1040	LP selection improves monotonically with $N$ ($+0.39$~pp at $N{=}8$ to $+0.95$~pp at $N{=}32$), but even at $N{=}32$, where degeneracy falls to 4\% and oracle headroom reaches ${\sim}20$~pp, the best signal captures less than 5\% of the available improvement.
1041	Degeneracy drops monotonically (43\% at $N{=}2$ to 4\% at $N{=}32$), yet reduced degeneracy does not unlock selection, consistent with the LoRA-rank ablation finding (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}).
1042	VC selection degrades with increasing $N$ ($-2.66$~pp at $N{=}2$ to $-4.35$~pp at $N{=}32$), consistent with mode-seeking bias in voting-based selection as the candidate pool grows.

1109	
1110	Oracle headroom increases monotonically with temperature ($+9.1$~pp at $T{=}0.5$ to $+14.6$~pp at $T{=}1.2$), confirming that higher temperature consistently expands the oracle ceiling under constrained structured decoding.
1111	Structural QE signals improve with temperature: SJ $\rho$ rises from $0.297$ ($T{=}0.5$) to $0.377$ ($T{=}1.2$), consistent with more diverse outputs providing richer pairwise evidence.
1112	LP $\rho$ peaks at $T{=}0.8$ ($0.253$) then degrades to $0.196$ at $T{=}1.2$.
1113	This divergence confirms that LP compression is model-intrinsic: higher temperature increases between-instance LP variance (benefiting structural signals) but does not resolve within-instance compression, degrading LP's discriminative power.
1114	Inter-sample agreement ($\kappa$) decreases monotonically ($0.577$ to $0.356$); higher temperature generates more diverse outputs. Mean sample F1 also decreases ($0.636$ to $0.594$), consistent with the quality--diversity trade-off.
1115	The $T{=}0.7$ and $T{=}1.0$ comparison in Table~\ref{tab:robustness} is consistent with the trends reported here.
1116	
1117	\paragraph{Temperature controls degeneracy and LP spread.}
1118	Table~\ref{tab:t_degeneracy} reports instance degeneracy (constant F1, gold-filtered) and median within-instance LP range across four temperatures on SciERC.
1119	Both quantities are strictly monotonic in $T$: degeneracy falls from 41.4\% ($T{=}0.5$) to 14.9\% ($T{=}1.2$), while LP range increases from 0.015 to 0.058 nats.
1120	Temperature thus provides a direct lever for practitioners to shift the degeneracy regime.
1121	However, even at $T{=}1.2$ where degeneracy (14.9\%) approaches Few-NERD levels (${\sim}$13\%), within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$ remains at 0.225, far below Few-NERD's ${\sim}$0.5.
1122	This confirms that temperature controls degeneracy but cannot improve LP-quality alignment, which is largely determined by task structure and remains relatively stable across generation configurations (\S\ref{app:epoch_confound}). LP-quality alignment, not degeneracy, is the primary predictor of LP selection direction (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).

1164	
1165	\paragraph{Wider-to-narrower schema transfer.}
1166	When the SciERC-trained model (6 NER types) is applied to CoNLL (4 types), all signals remain significant ($p{<}0.001$) and structural signals improve over their in-domain levels (same model, seed${=}42$, $N{=}8$; in-domain $\rho$ from Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): VC reaches $\rho{=}0.469$ (in-domain $0.379$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.090$), SJ $\rho{=}0.378$ (in-domain $0.360$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.018$), and FK $\rho{=}0.285$ (in-domain $0.267$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.018$).
1167	The wider training schema subsumes CoNLL's narrower schema, so the model produces reasonable outputs even out-of-domain (single-sample F1${=}0.522$), and structural signals benefit from the simpler target schema's reduced output ambiguity.
1168	
1169	\paragraph{Narrower-to-wider schema transfer (excluded).}
1170	The reverse direction is excluded from our conclusions due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure.
1171	The CoNLL-trained model recognizes only 4 news entity types (PER, ORG, LOC, MISC) and has no relation extraction capability; when applied to SciERC's 6 scientific entity types and 7 relation types, the model cannot produce meaningful structured outputs (single-sample NER F1${=}0.009$, RE F1${=}0.001$).
1172	Under this regime, most signals flip to negative or near-zero correlations (Table~\ref{tab:ood}); only VC retains weak positive correlation ($\rho{=}0.105$, $p{=}0.016$).
1173	These negative correlations reflect the model systematically generating confident but incorrect outputs, a capability failure analogous to WNUT-17 (\S\ref{sec:qe_results}) and not evidence against signal utility.
1174	
1175	\paragraph{Asymmetry interpretation.}
1176	The directional asymmetry is driven by schema coverage: wider$\to$narrower preserves signal utility because the SciERC model's 6-type schema subsumes CoNLL's 4 types, while narrower$\to$wider fails because the CoNLL model lacks the entity and relation types required by SciERC.
1177	This extends the capability boundary identified on WNUT-17 to the domain-shift setting: confidence signals require sufficient model capability as a prerequisite, and results from the CoNLL$\to$SciERC direction should not be interpreted as evidence of signal degradation under domain shift.
1178	
1179	\section{Verbalized Confidence}\label{app:verbalized_confidence}
1180	
1181	We evaluate verbalized confidence (VerbConf) as a second per-sample signal following \citet{lin2022teaching} and \citet{tian2023calibration}: after generating each structured output, the model is prompted to rate its confidence on a 1--10 scale.
1182	Table~\ref{tab:verbconf} reports quality estimation and selection results.

1189	\begin{tabular}{l cc cc}

1205	
1206	\paragraph{Severe overconfidence.}
1207	VerbConf exhibits extreme score compression: mean confidence is 8.58/10 on SciERC (single-sample F1${=}0.645$) and 9.24/10 on CoNLL (single-sample F1${=}0.904$), with most scores concentrated in the 8--10 range.
1208	This compression is more severe than LP (which spans a 0.041-nat median range); score compression is a general property of model self-assessment, not an artifact of log-probability computation.
1209	
1210	\paragraph{QE--selection decoupling.}
1211	VerbConf achieves higher $\rho$ than LP on both datasets (SciERC: 0.297 vs.\ 0.205; CoNLL: 0.337 vs.\ 0.316) and competitive AUROC (SciERC: 0.681 vs.\ 0.578; CoNLL: 0.747 vs.\ 0.753), yet yields the worst selection among all signals ($\Delta{=}{-}2.45$~pp on SciERC, $-1.93$~pp on CoNLL).
1212	This dissociation further demonstrates the correlation-selection gap: a signal can rank instances by expected quality without being able to discriminate candidates within an instance.
1213	The AUROC--$\rho$ decoupling on CoNLL (LP leads AUROC at 0.753 while VerbConf leads $\rho$ at 0.337) reflects differing sensitivity to binary vs.\ ordinal discrimination.

1248	Instance degeneracy is 13.0\% (vs.\ 17.8\% on SciERC, 61.7\% on CoNLL).

1270	\paragraph{Key observations.}
1271	(1)~All five signals achieve positive QE ($\rho$ 0.22--0.39); quality estimation works on Few-NERD. VC leads ($\rho{=}0.39$), followed by FK ($0.31$), SJ ($0.30$), EM ($0.29$), LP ($0.22$); the ranking matches SciERC where structural signals also outperform LP for quality estimation.
1272	(2)~LP is the only signal achieving significant positive selection ($+1.39$~pp, $p{<}0.001$, CI excludes zero). The sample-level AUROC of 0.578 confirms LP provides per-sample discriminability beyond instance-level quality prediction.
1273	(3)~Despite positive $\rho$, all structural signals degrade selection ($-1.79$ to $-3.85$~pp), further confirming that the correlation-selection gap is not an artifact of weak QE.
1274	(4)~Low degeneracy (13.0\%) combined with strong LP-quality alignment ($\rho{\approx}0.5$--$0.7$) enables LP selection: candidates differ in F1 more often, and LP can rank them effectively. This contrasts with SciERC (17.8\% degeneracy but weak alignment $\rho{\approx}0.24$, LP $+0.61$~pp) and CoNLL (61.7\%, LP $-0.85$~pp).

1280	The correlation between per-type degeneracy and oracle headroom is strong and significant (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.881$, $p{=}0.004$; exact permutation $p{=}0.005$); degeneracy directly constrains the headroom available for selection.

1345	
1346	\section{Calibration Baseline}\label{app:calibration_baseline}
1347	
1348	Standard calibration methods are monotonic transforms of log-probability and therefore preserve within-instance sample rankings: Platt scaling \cite{platt1999probabilistic} fits a logistic regression $\sigma(a \cdot \text{LP} + b)$, and temperature scaling \cite{guo2017calibration} divides logits by a scalar $\tau$.
1349	Since both preserve the argmax, selection F1 is identical to raw LP on all three datasets (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}).
1350	
1351	To break monotonicity, we fit a multi-feature Platt model (MF-Platt) using LP, token count, and entity count as inputs, trained on a held-out calibration split (20\% of the test set, 5-fold cross-validation on the remainder).
1352	MF-Platt can reorder candidates within an instance, yet yields no significant selection improvement: $\Delta{=}{-}1.1$~pp on SciERC ($p{=}0.72$), $+0.3$~pp on CoNLL ($p{=}0.31$), and $-0.6$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{=}0.83$).
1353	Platt scaling substantially improves probability calibration (ECE drops from 0.26--0.39 to 0.01--0.08), but better-calibrated probabilities do not translate to better selection.
1354	This parallels the supervised signal combination result (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}): neither stronger aggregation nor additional features can compensate for weak per-sample discriminative signal.
1355

1357	\centering

1360	\label{tab:calibration}

1374	\section{Epoch Confound Analysis}\label{app:epoch_confound}
1375	
1376	\paragraph{Training convergence modulates degeneracy.}
1377	Table~\ref{tab:epoch_confound} compares the 3-epoch model (used throughout the main text) with a 5-epoch variant on Few-NERD (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).
1378	Extended training increases greedy F1 by $+2.95$~pp (0.748 $\to$ 0.777) but nearly triples instance degeneracy (11.1\% $\to$ 31.3\%), reducing the fraction of instances with non-degenerate outputs from 85.6\% to 68.7\%.
1379	LP selection gains drop correspondingly ($+1.37$~pp $\to$ $+0.62$~pp), consistent with the degeneracy-gating mechanism identified in the main text (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
1380	Within-instance LP-quality alignment is relatively stable under this convergence shift (mean $\rho$: $0.524 \to 0.407$; median $\rho$: $0.667 \to 0.615$), consistent with alignment being determined primarily by task structure rather than training duration.
1381	
1382	Evaluation loss increases after epoch 3 (0.032 $\to$ 0.041 at epoch 5), and the best checkpoint by validation loss occurs at step 32{,}000 (approximately epoch 3.9).
1383	Standard early stopping practice thus naturally selects the low-degeneracy regime where LP selection is most effective.
1384	This finding refines the narrative: degeneracy is not a fixed property of datasets but is modulated by the degree of training convergence. Over-trained models compress their output distributions, collapsing diversity even on datasets that support it under well-regularized training.
1385

1387	\centering

The 3-epoch model corresponds to standard early stopping; the 5-epoch model is a controlled ablation.}

1397	Valid instances & 3{,}723 / 4{,}351 (85.6\%) & 2{,}988 / 4{,}351 (68.7\%) \\
1398	LP $\Delta$ (pp) & $+$1.37 & $+$0.62 \\
1399	Within-inst.\ $\rho$ (mean) & 0.524 & 0.407 \\
1400	Within-inst.\ $\rho$ (median) & 0.667 & 0.615 \\

1404	
1405	\paragraph{SciERC epoch curve.}
1406	Table~\ref{tab:epoch_scierc} extends the analysis to SciERC with a 3-point curve (3, 5, 10 epochs; Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).
1407	Degeneracy increases monotonically (6.4\% $\to$ 17.8\% $\to$ 47.8\%), and within-instance LP-quality correlation degrades in tandem (median $\rho$: 0.319 $\to$ 0.262 $\to$ 0.112; mean $\rho$: 0.273 $\to$ 0.179 $\to$ 0.066), confirming that overtraining erodes both diversity and LP alignment simultaneously.
1408	The fraction of instances with positive LP-quality correlation also declines (76.4\% at 3 epochs $\to$ 64.1\% at 5 epochs $\to$ 54.0\% at 10 epochs).
1409	LP selection does not yield significant gains at any training duration ($-1.24$, $+0.64$, $+1.02$~pp; all below the ${\approx}$1.5~pp MDE), despite oracle headroom ranging from 8.4 to 15.0~pp.
1410	The 3-epoch model has higher within-instance alignment ($\rho{=}0.319$) than the 5-epoch model ($\rho{=}0.262$), yet LP selection is worse ($-1.24$ vs.\ $+0.64$~pp): at 6.4\% degeneracy, more non-degenerate instances enter the candidate pool, but LP ranking precision is too low to exploit them, so the broader pool hurts rather than helps. This illustrates that low degeneracy does not ensure selection gains; LP-quality alignment determines selection direction.
1411	At 10 epochs, the model retains competitive greedy F1 (0.649 vs.\ 0.650 at 3 epochs) yet conditions are unfavorable: 47.8\% degenerate instances and $\rho{=}0.112$.
1412	The gradient confirms that degeneracy is convergence-driven rather than a fixed dataset property: extended training compresses output distributions, degrading both diversity and within-instance alignment. However, the epoch-to-epoch alignment variation ($\Delta\rho_{\text{median}}{=}0.057$) is small relative to the cross-dataset gap (SciERC ${\sim}$0.3 vs.\ Few-NERD ${\sim}$0.5), consistent with alignment being primarily dataset-intrinsic: training convergence modulates alignment at the margin but cannot shift a dataset into the strong-alignment regime.
1413	Even at the most favorable epoch (3-epoch, $\rho{=}0.319$), SciERC alignment remains below the ${\sim}$0.5 threshold associated with successful LP selection on Few-NERD.

1434	\paragraph{Limitations.}
1435	The Few-NERD epoch analysis covers 2 points (3 vs.\ 5 epochs); the SciERC curve provides a 3-point gradient showing monotonic degeneracy increase with simultaneous within-instance alignment degradation.
1436	CoNLL epoch variation remains unverified; high-degeneracy datasets (CoNLL at 59--89\%) may exhibit a ceiling effect where additional training has limited marginal impact.
1437	All Few-NERD results in the main text use the 3-epoch model (standard early stopping); the 5-epoch comparison is a controlled ablation.
1438	All SciERC results use the 5-epoch model; the 3- and 10-epoch variants serve as controlled ablations.
1439	
1440	\subsection{Degeneracy-Aware LP Selection}\label{app:dals}
1441	
1442	The non-tied subset analysis (Table~\ref{tab:nontied_main}) identifies within-instance LP range (max minus min LP across $N$ samples) as a per-instance degeneracy proxy: instances with near-zero LP range carry no discriminative signal.
1443	Degeneracy-aware LP selection (DALS) operationalizes this: when LP range exceeds $\tau{=}0.05$ (the threshold independently motivated by the non-tied analysis; Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}), select the highest-LP sample; otherwise fall back to greedy.
1444	On SciERC, DALS yields $+0.71{\pm}0.48$~pp (3-seed mean; individual seeds: $+0.19$ to $+1.14$~pp); on Few-NERD, $+0.30$~pp ($p{<}0.001$; gain remains positive across $\tau \in [0.01, 0.15]$); on CoNLL, where pure LP selection degrades F1 ($-0.85$~pp), DALS shows comparable degradation (Qwen: $-0.87$~pp; LLaMA: neutral).
1445	DALS improves over pure LP selection when degeneracy is moderate, provides partial damage control when LP selection is harmful, and defaults to greedy when LP range signals no information.
1446	While gains are modest and seed-sensitive, LP range serves as an operationally accessible degeneracy proxy, though the mixed cross-dataset results confirm that degeneracy gating alone cannot ensure positive selection outcomes.
1447	
1448	\paragraph{LOO $\tau$ sensitivity.}
1449	Leave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation across datasets reveals that the optimal $\tau$ spans two orders of magnitude: $\tau^*{=}0.001$ on Few-NERD, $0.05$ on SciERC, and $0.10$ on CoNLL.
1450	LOO regret (the gap between oracle and LOO-selected $\tau$) ranges from 0.51 to 1.21~pp.
1451	The best single cross-dataset $\tau$ ($\tau{=}0.05$) matches the independently motivated non-tied threshold, but yields dataset-specific suboptimality: even with oracle $\tau$, CoNLL remains at $-0.31$~pp.
1452	This sensitivity reinforces the diagnostic framing of DALS: per-dataset $\tau$ tuning is required for positive gains, and even optimally tuned DALS cannot overcome weak LP-quality alignment on high-degeneracy datasets.
1453

1457	Table~\ref{tab:conditional_decomp} reports results across three datasets.

1466	Dataset & LP $\Delta$ (pp) & 95\% CI & Interpretation \\
1467	\midrule
1468	SciERC & $+$2.47 & [$+$0.30, $+$4.70] & significant positive \\
1469	Few-NERD & $+$0.63 & [$-$0.02, $+$1.30] & marginal positive \\
1470	CoNLL & $-$1.78 & [$-$2.83, $-$0.76] & significant negative \\
1471	\bottomrule
1472	\end{tabular}
1473	\end{table}
1474	
1475	On SciERC, restricting to the favorable subset recovers a significant LP gain, consistent with LP-quality alignment driving selection within the non-degenerate pool.
1476	On Few-NERD, the trend is directionally consistent but the confidence interval crosses zero; the full-dataset result ($+1.39$~pp, $p{<}0.001$) reflects the larger sample size rather than a stronger per-instance effect.
1477	CoNLL remains negative even on the favorable subset, consistent with the ceiling-regime interpretation: at $>$90\% greedy F1, residual errors are idiosyncratic and LP cannot rank corrections.
1478	
1479	\subsection{Entity-Token LP Decomposition}\label{app:entity_token_lp}
1480	
1481	Full-sequence LP averages over all output tokens, diluting entity-relevant signal with formatting and schema tokens.
1482	Entity-token LP restricts the average to tokens within entity text and type spans, yielding $4{\times}$ higher within-instance variance (median 0.000644 vs.\ 0.000161).
1483	Table~\ref{tab:entity_token_lp} compares the two aggregation strategies on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).
1484

1487	\small

1489	\label{tab:entity_token_lp}

1492	Aggregation & Med.\ var. & $\rho$ & AUROC & $\Delta$ (pp) \\

Values differ slightly from Table~\\ref{tab:selection} (0.645) and Table~\\ref{tab:fewnerd} (0.644) due to seed and aggregation differences; relative trends are consistent.}\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap significance at $N{=}32$.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_n32} reports bootstrap confidence intervals and $p$-values for selection at $N{=}32$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, $n{=}529$).\nNo signal achieves significant improvement over greedy at $\\alpha{=}0.05$ (LP: $p{=}0.091$; FK: $p{=}0.101$), while VC is significantly \\textit{worse} ($-4.36$~pp, $p{=}1.0$).\nHowever, all signals except VC significantly outperform random selection ($p{<}0.001$, permutation test); the signals carry meaningful quality information yet fail to translate it into reliable selection gains.\nThe oracle gap ($+20.0$~pp, $p{<}0.001$) confirms substantial unrealized headroom.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap significance test at $N{=}32$ (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, $T{=}1.0$, $B{=}10{,}000$, $n{=}529$ gold-non-empty instances; 22 instances with no gold entities are excluded as they contribute zero selection differential). $\\Delta$: selection gain vs.\\ greedy. CI: 95\\% bootstrap percentile interval. $p_{\\text{greedy}}$: one-sided $p$-value vs.\\ greedy; $p_{\\text{rand}}$: permutation test vs.\\ random selection.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap_n32}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\Delta$ (pp) & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{greedy}}$ & $p_{\\text{rand}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & $+$0.95 & [$-$0.41, $+$2.34] & 0.091 & ${<}$0.001 \\\\\nFK & $+$0.66 & [$-$0.36, $+$1.67] & 0.101 & ${<}$0.001 \\\\\nSJ & $+$0.36 & [$-$0.66, $+$1.38] & 0.244 & ${<}$0.001 \\\\\nEM & $+$0.22 & [$-$0.99, $+$1.44] & 0.355 & ${<}$0.001 \\\\\nVC & $-$4.36 & [$-$6.26, $-$2.37] & 1.000 & 0.741 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $+$20.00 & [$+$17.98, $+$22.10] & ${<}$0.001 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Temperature Ablation}\\label{app:t_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:t_ablation} reports the effect of sampling temperature on QE signals, oracle headroom, and output quality across four temperatures on SciERC NER.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Temperature ablation on SciERC NER (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$). Oracle gap and QE $\\rho$ across four temperatures. $\\kappa$: Fleiss' $\\kappa$ (inter-sample agreement). $T{=}0.5$ and $T{=}1.0$ use joint (NER+RE) subtask; $T{=}0.8$ and $T{=}1.2$ use NER-only.}\n\\label{tab:t_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ccccccc}\n\\toprule\n$T$ & Oracle gap (pp) & SJ $\\rho$ & LP $\\rho$ & $\\kappa$ & Sample F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & $+$9.1 & 0.297 & 0.218 & 0.577 & 0.636 \\\\\n0.8 & $+$12.5 & 0.359 & 0.253 & 0.451 & 0.622 \\\\\n1.0 & $+$13.0 & 0.360 & 0.205 & ${\\approx}$0.39 & ${\\approx}$0.612 \\\\\n1.2 & $+$14.6 & 0.377 & 0.196 & 0.356 & 0.594 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOracle headroom increases monotonically with temperature ($+9.1$~pp at $T{=}0.5$ to $+14.6$~pp at $T{=}1.2$), confirming that higher temperature consistently expands the oracle ceiling under constrained structured decoding.\nStructural QE signals improve with temperature: SJ $\\rho$ rises from $0.297$ ($T{=}0.5$) to $0.377$ ($T{=}1.2$), consistent with more diverse outputs providing richer pairwise evidence.\nLP $\\rho$ peaks at $T{=}0.8$ ($0.253$) then degrades to $0.196$ at $T{=}1.2$.\nThis divergence confirms that LP compression is model-intrinsic: higher temperature increases between-instance LP variance (benefiting structural signals) but does not resolve within-instance compression, degrading LP's discriminative power.\nInter-sample agreement ($\\kappa$) decreases monotonically ($0.577$ to $0.356$); higher temperature generates more diverse outputs. Mean sample F1 also decreases ($0.636$ to $0.594$), consistent with the quality--diversity trade-off.\nThe $T{=}0.7$ and $T{=}1.0$ comparison in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness} is consistent with the trends reported here.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature controls degeneracy and LP spread.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:t_degeneracy} reports instance degeneracy (constant F1, gold-filtered) and median within-instance LP range across four temperatures on SciERC.\nBoth quantities are strictly monotonic in $T$: degeneracy falls from 41.4\\% ($T{=}0.5$) to 14.9\\% ($T{=}1.2$), while LP range increases from 0.015 to 0.058 nats.\nTemperature thus provides a direct lever for practitioners to shift the degeneracy regime.\nHowever, even at $T{=}1.2$ where degeneracy (14.9\\%) approaches Few-NERD levels (${\\sim}$13\\%), within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$ remains at 0.225, far below Few-NERD's ${\\sim}$0.5.\nThis confirms that temperature controls degeneracy but cannot improve LP-quality alignment, which is largely determined by task structure and remains relatively stable across generation configurations (\\S\\ref{app:epoch_confound}). LP-quality alignment, not degeneracy, is the primary predictor of LP selection direction (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Temperature sensitivity of degeneracy and LP spread (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$). Degeneracy: constant-F1, gold-filtered. LP range: median within-instance range.}\n\\label{tab:t_degeneracy}\n\\begin{tabular}{ccccc}\n\\toprule\n$T$ & Degen.\\% & LP range (nats) & Fleiss' $\\kappa$ & SJ mean \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 41.4 & 0.015 & 0.572 & 0.830 \\\\\n0.8 & 23.8 & 0.031 & 0.471 & 0.752 \\\\\n1.0 & 17.8 & 0.042 & 0.405 & 0.700 \\\\\n1.2 & 14.9 & 0.058 & 0.352 & 0.647 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Dataset OOD Analysis}\\label{app:ood}\n\nTo test whether confidence signals generalize under domain shift, we evaluate models on out-of-distribution data: a SciERC-trained model applied to CoNLL instances (wider$\\to$narrower schema) and vice versa (Table~\\ref{tab:ood}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-dataset OOD quality estimation (Spearman $\\rho$, Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). SciERC$\\to$CoNLL: SciERC-trained model on CoNLL instances ($n{=}2756$). CoNLL$\\to$SciERC: CoNLL-trained model on SciERC instances ($n{=}529$, excluded from conclusions due to catastrophic model failure). In-domain $\\rho$ from Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} (same model, same seed). \\textsuperscript{***}$p{<}0.001$, \\textsuperscript{*}$p{<}0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:ood}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC$\\to$CoNLL}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL$\\to$SciERC}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\nSignal & $\\rho$ & In-dom. & $\\Delta$ & $\\rho$ & In-dom. & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & .469\\textsuperscript{***} & .379 & +.090 & +.105\\textsuperscript{*} & .379 & $-$.274 \\\\\nSJ & .378\\textsuperscript{***} & .360 & +.018 & $-$.186\\textsuperscript{***} & .360 & $-$.546 \\\\\nEM & .300\\textsuperscript{***} & .295 & +.005 & $-$.202\\textsuperscript{***} & .295 & $-$.497 \\\\\nFK & .285\\textsuperscript{***} & .267 & +.018 & $-$.070 & .267 & $-$.337 \\\\\nLP & .246\\textsuperscript{***} & .205 & +.041 & $-$.152\\textsuperscript{***} & .205 & $-$.357 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Wider-to-narrower schema transfer.}\nWhen the SciERC-trained model (6 NER types) is applied to CoNLL (4 types), all signals remain significant ($p{<}0.001$) and structural signals improve over their in-domain levels (same model, seed${=}42$, $N{=}8$; in-domain $\\rho$ from Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): VC reaches $\\rho{=}0.469$ (in-domain $0.379$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.090$), SJ $\\rho{=}0.378$ (in-domain $0.360$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.018$), and FK $\\rho{=}0.285$ (in-domain $0.267$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.018$).\nThe wider training schema subsumes CoNLL's narrower schema, so the model produces reasonable outputs even out-of-domain (single-sample F1${=}0.522$), and structural signals benefit from the simpler target schema's reduced output ambiguity.\n\n\\paragraph{Narrower-to-wider schema transfer (excluded).}\nThe reverse direction is excluded from our conclusions due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure.\nThe CoNLL-trained model recognizes only 4 news entity types (PER, ORG, LOC, MISC) and has no relation extraction capability; when applied to SciERC's 6 scientific entity types and 7 relation types, the model cannot produce meaningful structured outputs (single-sample NER F1${=}0.009$, RE F1${=}0.001$).\nUnder this regime, most signals flip to negative or near-zero correlations (Table~\\ref{tab:ood}); only VC retains weak positive correlation ($\\rho{=}0.105$, $p{=}0.016$).\nThese negative correlations reflect the model systematically generating confident but incorrect outputs, a capability failure analogous to WNUT-17 (\\S\\ref{sec:qe_results}) and not evidence against signal utility.\n\n\\paragraph{Asymmetry interpretation.}\nThe directional asymmetry is driven by schema coverage: wider$\\to$narrower preserves signal utility because the SciERC model's 6-type schema subsumes CoNLL's 4 types, while narrower$\\to$wider fails because the CoNLL model lacks the entity and relation types required by SciERC.\nThis extends the capability boundary identified on WNUT-17 to the domain-shift setting: confidence signals require sufficient model capability as a prerequisite, and results from the CoNLL$\\to$SciERC direction should not be interpreted as evidence of signal degradation under domain shift.\n\n\\section{Verbalized Confidence}\\label{app:verbalized_confidence}\n\nWe evaluate verbalized confidence (VerbConf) as a second per-sample signal following \\citet{lin2022teaching} and \\citet{tian2023calibration}: after generating each structured output, the model is prompted to rate its confidence on a 1--10 scale.\nTable~\\ref{tab:verbconf} reports quality estimation and selection results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Verbalized confidence (VerbConf) vs.\\ log-probability selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). Parse fail: fraction of samples where the confidence score could not be extracted.}\n\\label{tab:verbconf}\n\\begin{tabular}{l cc cc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL NER}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nMetric & VerbConf & LP & VerbConf & LP \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\rho$ & 0.297 & 0.205 & 0.337 & 0.316 \\\\\nAUROC & 0.681 & 0.578 & 0.747 & 0.753 \\\\\nSelection F1 & 0.620 & 0.647 & 0.889 & 0.904 \\\\\n$\\Delta$ vs.\\ greedy & $-$2.45\\,pp & $+$0.2\\,pp & $-$1.93\\,pp & $-$0.77\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean score & 8.58\\,/\\,10 & --- & 9.24\\,/\\,10 & --- \\\\\nParse fail rate & 0.26\\% & --- & 0\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Severe overconfidence.}\nVerbConf exhibits extreme score compression: mean confidence is 8.58/10 on SciERC (single-sample F1${=}0.645$) and 9.24/10 on CoNLL (single-sample F1${=}0.904$), with most scores concentrated in the 8--10 range.\nThis compression is more severe than LP (which spans a 0.041-nat median range); score compression is a general property of model self-assessment, not an artifact of log-probability computation.\n\n\\paragraph{QE--selection decoupling.}\nVerbConf achieves higher $\\rho$ than LP on both datasets (SciERC: 0.297 vs.\\ 0.205; CoNLL: 0.337 vs.\\ 0.316) and competitive AUROC (SciERC: 0.681 vs.\\ 0.578; CoNLL: 0.747 vs.\\ 0.753), yet yields the worst selection among all signals ($\\Delta{=}{-}2.45$~pp on SciERC, $-1.93$~pp on CoNLL).\nThis dissociation further demonstrates the correlation-selection gap: a signal can rank instances by expected quality without being able to discriminate candidates within an instance.\nThe AUROC--$\\rho$ decoupling on CoNLL (LP leads AUROC at 0.753 while VerbConf leads $\\rho$ at 0.337) reflects differing sensitivity to binary vs.\\ ordinal discrimination.\n\n\\section{LLaMA-3.2-3B Scale Ablation}\\label{app:llama3b}\n\nTo extend scale coverage below 4B, we fine-tune LLaMA-3.2-3B-Instruct on SciERC NER under identical training conditions (LoRA rank${=}32$, $\\alpha{=}64$, 5 epochs) and evaluate quality estimation and selection (Table~\\ref{tab:llama3b}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{LLaMA-3.2-3B SciERC NER quality estimation and selection ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). Greedy F1${=}0.627$, Oracle F1${=}0.763$, headroom${=}13.63$~pp.}\n\\label{tab:llama3b}\n\\begin{tabular}{l cc cc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & AUROC & Sel.\\ F1 & $\\Delta$ vs.\\ greedy \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & \\textbf{.460} & .743 & 0.611 & $-$1.54\\,pp \\\\\nSJ & .375 & .738 & \\textbf{0.633} & $+$0.61\\,pp \\\\\nEM & .334 & \\textbf{.776} & --- & --- \\\\\nFK & .268 & .671 & 0.630 & $+$0.28\\,pp \\\\\nLP & .266 & .633 & 0.628 & $+$0.12\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy & --- & --- & 0.627 & --- \\\\\nOracle & --- & --- & 0.763 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe $\\rho$ ranking at 3B (VC $>$ SJ $>$ EM $>$ FK $\\approx$ LP) preserves the structural-over-LP ordering from 8B (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}); ranking stability holds across two model families spanning 3B--8B.\nAUROC rankings differ (EM $>$ VC $>$ SJ $>$ FK $>$ LP), consistent with the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation noted in the main text.\nDespite 13.63~pp of oracle headroom, no signal achieves significant selection improvement (best: SJ, $+0.61$~pp, $p{=}0.175$); VC, the strongest QE signal, yields the worst selection ($-1.54$~pp), consistent with the correlation-selection gap observed at larger scales.\n\n\\section{Few-NERD Full Results}\\label{app:fewnerd}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:fewnerd_full} reports the complete Few-NERD INTRA coarse evaluation (Qwen3-8B, LoRA rank${=}32$, 3 epochs, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).\nAfter gold-filtering (removing instances with no gold entities), the evaluation set contains $n{=}32{,}565$ instances across 8 coarse entity types; 11.9\\% receive zero F1 under greedy decoding.\nInstance degeneracy is 13.0\\% (vs.\\ 17.8\\% on SciERC, 61.7\\% on CoNLL).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Few-NERD INTRA coarse: complete quality estimation and selection results (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, gold-filtered $n{=}32{,}565$). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation with instance F1. Sample AUROC: per-sample LP discrimination. Sel.\\ $\\Delta$: selection F1 minus greedy (0.748). CI: bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval.}\n\\label{tab:fewnerd_full}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & Sample AUROC & Sel.\\ $\\Delta$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP & .220 & .578 & $\\mathbf{+1.39}$\\,pp & [+1.18, +1.53] \\\\\nVC & .386 & --- & $-$1.79\\,pp & --- \\\\\nFK & .313 & --- & $-$2.26\\,pp & --- \\\\\nSJ & .300 & --- & $-$2.14\\,pp & --- \\\\\nEM & .287 & --- & $-$3.85\\,pp & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{Greedy: 0.748 \\quad Oracle: 0.872} & \\multicolumn{2}{l}{Headroom: 12.4\\,pp} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Key observations.}\n(1)~All five signals achieve positive QE ($\\rho$ 0.22--0.39); quality estimation works on Few-NERD. VC leads ($\\rho{=}0.39$), followed by FK ($0.31$), SJ ($0.30$), EM ($0.29$), LP ($0.22$); the ranking matches SciERC where structural signals also outperform LP for quality estimation.\n(2)~LP is the only signal achieving significant positive selection ($+1.39$~pp, $p{<}0.001$, CI excludes zero). The sample-level AUROC of 0.578 confirms LP provides per-sample discriminability beyond instance-level quality prediction.\n(3)~Despite positive $\\rho$, all structural signals degrade selection ($-1.79$ to $-3.85$~pp), further confirming that the correlation-selection gap is not an artifact of weak QE.\n(4)~Low degeneracy (13.0\\%) combined with strong LP-quality alignment ($\\rho{\\approx}0.5$--$0.7$) enables LP selection: candidates differ in F1 more often, and LP can rank them effectively. This contrasts with SciERC (17.8\\% degeneracy but weak alignment $\\rho{\\approx}0.24$, LP $+0.61$~pp) and CoNLL (61.7\\%, LP $-0.85$~pp).\n\n\\section{Per-Entity-Type LP Selection Analysis}\\label{app:per_type_lp}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_type_lp} stratifies LP selection by entity type on Few-NERD, sorted by degeneracy rate.\nThe degeneracy-gating mechanism holds at entity-type granularity: 7 of 8 types show positive LP selection gains, and the sole negative type (Art, $-0.17$~pp) has the highest degeneracy (19.8\\%).\nThe correlation between per-type degeneracy and oracle headroom is strong and significant (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.881$, $p{=}0.004$; exact permutation $p{=}0.005$); degeneracy directly constrains the headroom available for selection.\nUnder a conservative $\\text{df}{=}4$ adjustment for within-type dependence, the correlation remains significant ($p{=}0.033$).\nEntity types show near-zero intra-class correlation (ICC $= -0.004$), supporting the independence assumption underlying per-type analysis.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-entity-type LP selection on Few-NERD INTRA coarse (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), sorted by degeneracy. LP $\\Delta$: LP selection F1 minus greedy. 7/8 types show positive $\\Delta$; Art (highest degeneracy) is the sole exception.}\n\\label{tab:per_type_lp}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nEntity Type & $n$ & Degen.\\% & Greedy & LP Sel & $\\Delta$ (pp) & LP $\\rho$ & Headroom \\\\\n\\midrule\nEvent & 1{,}306 & 6.0 & .655 & .662 & $+$0.65 & .457 & 16.1\\,pp \\\\\nOther & 3{,}433 & 6.6 & .637 & .685 & $\\mathbf{+4.73}$ & .491 & 19.4\\,pp \\\\\nOrganization & 6{,}647 & 6.7 & .720 & .732 & $+$1.21 & .509 & 13.3\\,pp \\\\\nBuilding & 1{,}587 & 7.2 & .702 & .705 & $+$0.23 & .478 & 14.9\\,pp \\\\\nProduct & 2{,}276 & 8.2 & .656 & .677 & $+$2.01 & .483 & 15.8\\,pp \\\\\nLocation & 8{,}023 & 11.4 & .793 & .798 & $+$0.53 & .520 & 10.3\\,pp \\\\\nPerson & 8{,}016 & 13.4 & .822 & .835 & $+$1.25 & .544 & 9.2\\,pp \\\\\nArt & 1{,}277 & 19.8 & .756 & .754 & $-$0.17 & .451 & 9.2\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{CoNLL-2003 per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}16$, $T{=}1.0$). All types exceed 20\\% degeneracy and show negative LP selection, consistent with the dataset's ceiling-regime performance (F1 $>$ 90\\%).}\n\\label{tab:per_type_lp_conll}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nEntity Type & Degen.\\% & LP $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMISC & 23.2 & $-$2.19 \\\\\nPER & 42.6 & $-$1.08 \\\\\nLOC & 47.5 & $-$0.62 \\\\\nORG & 54.3 & $-$0.55 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SciERC per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Within-dataset, degeneracy alone does not predict LP effectiveness (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.14$, $p{=}0.79$); additional type-specific factors likely modulate selection at this granularity.}\n\\label{tab:per_type_lp_scierc}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nEntity Type & Degen.\\% & LP $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTask & 10.1 & $+$2.58 \\\\\nGeneric & 10.0 & $+$2.07 \\\\\nMaterial & 20.0 & $+$0.91 \\\\\nMetric & 12.5 & $-$0.06 \\\\\nMethod & 16.3 & $-$0.03 \\\\\nOtherScientificTerm & 6.2 & $-$0.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe cross-dataset degeneracy gradient (61.7\\% $\\to$ 17.8\\% $\\to$ 13.0\\%) is inversely associated with LP selection effectiveness at the macro level ($n{=}3$ datasets; exploratory).\\footnote{Partial Spearman correlation controlling for entity-type frequency yields $\\rho{=}{-}0.47$ (vs.\\ raw $\\rho{=}{-}0.38$); type frequency does not confound the degeneracy--LP relationship.}\nAt finer per-type granularity, selection depends on the interaction of degeneracy, oracle headroom, and type-specific LP-quality alignment.\nA Kruskal-Wallis test finds no significant per-type heterogeneity in LP selection gains on SciERC ($p{=}0.796$), consistent with the small per-type sample sizes ($n{=}54$--$296$).\nLow degeneracy alone does not ensure selection gains: CoNLL MISC (23\\%, headroom 12.6~pp) and SciERC OtherScientificTerm (6.2\\%, headroom 12.5~pp) both show negative LP selection despite available headroom; LP correlation strength with the type-specific quality function is an additional prerequisite.\n\n\\section{Calibration Baseline}\\label{app:calibration_baseline}\n\nStandard calibration methods are monotonic transforms of log-probability and therefore preserve within-instance sample rankings: Platt scaling \\cite{platt1999probabilistic} fits a logistic regression $\\sigma(a \\cdot \\text{LP} + b)$, and temperature scaling \\cite{guo2017calibration} divides logits by a scalar $\\tau$.\nSince both preserve the argmax, selection F1 is identical to raw LP on all three datasets (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}).\n\nTo break monotonicity, we fit a multi-feature Platt model (MF-Platt) using LP, token count, and entity count as inputs, trained on a held-out calibration split (20\\% of the test set, 5-fold cross-validation on the remainder).\nMF-Platt can reorder candidates within an instance, yet yields no significant selection improvement: $\\Delta{=}{-}1.1$~pp on SciERC ($p{=}0.72$), $+0.3$~pp on CoNLL ($p{=}0.31$), and $-0.6$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{=}0.83$).\nPlatt scaling substantially improves probability calibration (ECE drops from 0.26--0.39 to 0.01--0.08), but better-calibrated probabilities do not translate to better selection.\nThis parallels the supervised signal combination result (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_main}): neither stronger aggregation nor additional features can compensate for weak per-sample discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Calibration baseline results ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Platt and TempScale are monotonic transforms of LP that preserve the within-instance argmax (selection F1 $\\equiv$ Raw LP, omitted). MF-Platt uses LP $+$ token count $+$ entity count; $p$: bootstrap test vs.\\ Raw LP.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{l cccc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Selection F1} & & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{ECE} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\nDataset & Greedy & Raw LP & MF-Platt ($p$) & & Raw & Platt \\\\\n\\midrule\nSciERC & .644 & .650 & .639 (.72) & & .260 & .048 \\\\\nCoNLL & .909 & .898 & .901 (.31) & & .392 & .012 \\\\\nFew-NERD & .771 & .785 & .778 (.83) & & .262 & .080 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Epoch Confound Analysis}\\label{app:epoch_confound}\n\n\\paragraph{Training convergence modulates degeneracy.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:epoch_confound} compares the 3-epoch model (used throughout the main text) with a 5-epoch variant on Few-NERD (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).\nExtended training increases greedy F1 by $+2.95$~pp (0.748 $\\to$ 0.777) but nearly triples instance degeneracy (11.1\\% $\\to$ 31.3\\%), reducing the fraction of instances with non-degenerate outputs from 85.6\\% to 68.7\\%.\nLP selection gains drop correspondingly ($+1.37$~pp $\\to$ $+0.62$~pp), consistent with the degeneracy-gating mechanism identified in the main text (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nWithin-instance LP-quality alignment is relatively stable under this convergence shift (mean $\\rho$: $0.524 \\to 0.407$; median $\\rho$: $0.667 \\to 0.615$), consistent with alignment being determined primarily by task structure rather than training duration.\n\nEvaluation loss increases after epoch 3 (0.032 $\\to$ 0.041 at epoch 5), and the best checkpoint by validation loss occurs at step 32{,}000 (approximately epoch 3.9).\nStandard early stopping practice thus naturally selects the low-degeneracy regime where LP selection is most effective.\nThis finding refines the narrative: degeneracy is not a fixed property of datasets but is modulated by the degree of training convergence. Over-trained models compress their output distributions, collapsing diversity even on datasets that support it under well-regularized training.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Training convergence confound on Few-NERD (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Evaluated on the exp\\_027 5K subsample (gold-filtered $n{=}4{,}351$); main-table results (Table~\\ref{tab:fewnerd}) use the full test set ($n{=}32{,}565$, gold-filtered), yielding slightly different degeneracy (13.8\\% vs.\\ 11.1\\% here). ``Degen.'': constant-F1 instance degeneracy (gold-filtered). ``Valid'': fraction of instances with non-degenerate outputs. ``$\\rho$ mean/med'': within-instance LP-quality correlation. The 3-epoch model corresponds to standard early stopping; the 5-epoch model is a controlled ablation.}\n\\label{tab:epoch_confound}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & 3-epoch & 5-epoch \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy F1 & 0.748 & 0.777 \\\\\nInstance degen.\\ (\\%) & 11.1 & 31.3 \\\\\nValid instances & 3{,}723 / 4{,}351 (85.6\\%) & 2{,}988 / 4{,}351 (68.7\\%) \\\\\nLP $\\Delta$ (pp) & $+$1.37 & $+$0.62 \\\\\nWithin-inst.\\ $\\rho$ (mean) & 0.524 & 0.407 \\\\\nWithin-inst.\\ $\\rho$ (median) & 0.667 & 0.615 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{SciERC epoch curve.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:epoch_scierc} extends the analysis to SciERC with a 3-point curve (3, 5, 10 epochs; Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).\nDegeneracy increases monotonically (6.4\\% $\\to$ 17.8\\% $\\to$ 47.8\\%), and within-instance LP-quality correlation degrades in tandem (median $\\rho$: 0.319 $\\to$ 0.262 $\\to$ 0.112; mean $\\rho$: 0.273 $\\to$ 0.179 $\\to$ 0.066), confirming that overtraining erodes both diversity and LP alignment simultaneously.\nThe fraction of instances with positive LP-quality correlation also declines (76.4\\% at 3 epochs $\\to$ 64.1\\% at 5 epochs $\\to$ 54.0\\% at 10 epochs).\nLP selection does not yield significant gains at any training duration ($-1.24$, $+0.64$, $+1.02$~pp; all below the ${\\approx}$1.5~pp MDE), despite oracle headroom ranging from 8.4 to 15.0~pp.\nThe 3-epoch model has higher within-instance alignment ($\\rho{=}0.319$) than the 5-epoch model ($\\rho{=}0.262$), yet LP selection is worse ($-1.24$ vs.\\ $+0.64$~pp): at 6.4\\% degeneracy, more non-degenerate instances enter the candidate pool, but LP ranking precision is too low to exploit them, so the broader pool hurts rather than helps. This illustrates that low degeneracy does not ensure selection gains; LP-quality alignment determines selection direction.\nAt 10 epochs, the model retains competitive greedy F1 (0.649 vs.\\ 0.650 at 3 epochs) yet conditions are unfavorable: 47.8\\% degenerate instances and $\\rho{=}0.112$.\nThe gradient confirms that degeneracy is convergence-driven rather than a fixed dataset property: extended training compresses output distributions, degrading both diversity and within-instance alignment. However, the epoch-to-epoch alignment variation ($\\Delta\\rho_{\\text{median}}{=}0.057$) is small relative to the cross-dataset gap (SciERC ${\\sim}$0.3 vs.\\ Few-NERD ${\\sim}$0.5), consistent with alignment being primarily dataset-intrinsic: training convergence modulates alignment at the margin but cannot shift a dataset into the strong-alignment regime.\nEven at the most favorable epoch (3-epoch, $\\rho{=}0.319$), SciERC alignment remains below the ${\\sim}$0.5 threshold associated with successful LP selection on Few-NERD.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SciERC NER epoch curve (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Training duration modulates both instance degeneracy and within-instance LP-quality alignment. ``Degen.'': constant-F1 instance degeneracy (gold-filtered). ``LP $\\Delta$'': LP selection gain over greedy (gold-filtered; none reaches the ${\\approx}$1.5~pp MDE). ``$\\rho$ med'': median within-instance LP-quality correlation. Greedy F1 uses span-based evaluation consistent with Tables 1--3; $\\rho$ and LP $\\Delta$ use text-based matching consistent with Table~\\ref{tab:within_rho}.}\n\\label{tab:epoch_scierc}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & 3-epoch & 5-epoch & 10-epoch \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy F1 & 0.650 & 0.644 & 0.649 \\\\\nInstance degen.\\ (\\%) & 6.4 & 17.8 & 47.8 \\\\\nLP $\\Delta$ (pp) & $-$1.24 & $+$0.64 & $+$1.02 \\\\\nWithin-inst.\\ $\\rho$ (median) & 0.319 & 0.262 & 0.112 \\\\\nWithin-inst.\\ $\\rho$ (mean) & 0.273 & 0.179 & 0.066 \\\\\n\\% positive $\\rho$ & 76.4 & 64.1 & 54.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nThe Few-NERD epoch analysis covers 2 points (3 vs.\\ 5 epochs); the SciERC curve provides a 3-point gradient showing monotonic degeneracy increase with simultaneous within-instance alignment degradation.\nCoNLL epoch variation remains unverified; high-degeneracy datasets (CoNLL at 59--89\\%) may exhibit a ceiling effect where additional training has limited marginal impact.\nAll Few-NERD results in the main text use the 3-epoch model (standard early stopping); the 5-epoch comparison is a controlled ablation.\nAll SciERC results use the 5-epoch model; the 3- and 10-epoch variants serve as controlled ablations.\n\n\\subsection{Degeneracy-Aware LP Selection}\\label{app:dals}\n\nThe non-tied subset analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:nontied_main}) identifies within-instance LP range (max minus min LP across $N$ samples) as a per-instance degeneracy proxy: instances with near-zero LP range carry no discriminative signal.\nDegeneracy-aware LP selection (DALS) operationalizes this: when LP range exceeds $\\tau{=}0.05$ (the threshold independently motivated by the non-tied analysis; Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}), select the highest-LP sample; otherwise fall back to greedy.\nOn SciERC, DALS yields $+0.71{\\pm}0.48$~pp (3-seed mean; individual seeds: $+0.19$ to $+1.14$~pp); on Few-NERD, $+0.30$~pp ($p{<}0.001$; gain remains positive across $\\tau \\in [0.01, 0.15]$); on CoNLL, where pure LP selection degrades F1 ($-0.85$~pp), DALS shows comparable degradation (Qwen: $-0.87$~pp; LLaMA: neutral).\nDALS improves over pure LP selection when degeneracy is moderate, provides partial damage control when LP selection is harmful, and defaults to greedy when LP range signals no information.\nWhile gains are modest and seed-sensitive, LP range serves as an operationally accessible degeneracy proxy, though the mixed cross-dataset results confirm that degeneracy gating alone cannot ensure positive selection outcomes.\n\n\\paragraph{LOO $\\tau$ sensitivity.}\nLeave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation across datasets reveals that the optimal $\\tau$ spans two orders of magnitude: $\\tau^*{=}0.001$ on Few-NERD, $0.05$ on SciERC, and $0.10$ on CoNLL.\nLOO regret (the gap between oracle and LOO-selected $\\tau$) ranges from 0.51 to 1.21~pp.\nThe best single cross-dataset $\\tau$ ($\\tau{=}0.05$) matches the independently motivated non-tied threshold, but yields dataset-specific suboptimality: even with oracle $\\tau$, CoNLL remains at $-0.31$~pp.\nThis sensitivity reinforces the diagnostic framing of DALS: per-dataset $\\tau$ tuning is required for positive gains, and even optimally tuned DALS cannot overcome weak LP-quality alignment on high-degeneracy datasets.\n\n\\subsection{Conditional Decomposition of LP Selection}\\label{app:conditional_decomp}\n\nTo test whether LP-quality alignment drives selection gains within datasets, we restrict LP selection to the favorable subset: non-degenerate instances whose within-instance LP range exceeds the dataset-specific median (SciERC: 0.040 nats, CoNLL: 0.062, Few-NERD: 0.067).\nTable~\\ref{tab:conditional_decomp} reports results across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{LP selection on the favorable subset (non-degenerate instances with LP range above the dataset-specific median) per dataset (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). CI: bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval.}\n\\label{tab:conditional_decomp}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & LP $\\Delta$ (pp) & 95\\% CI & Interpretation \\\\\n\\midrule\nSciERC & $+$2.47 & [$+$0.30, $+$4.70] & significant positive \\\\\nFew-NERD & $+$0.63 & [$-$0.02, $+$1.30] & marginal positive \\\\\nCoNLL & $-$1.78 & [$-$2.83, $-$0.76] & significant negative \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn SciERC, restricting to the favorable subset recovers a significant LP gain, consistent with LP-quality alignment driving selection within the non-degenerate pool.\nOn Few-NERD, the trend is directionally consistent but the confidence interval crosses zero; the full-dataset result ($+1.39$~pp, $p{<}0.001$) reflects the larger sample size rather than a stronger per-instance effect.\nCoNLL remains negative even on the favorable subset, consistent with the ceiling-regime interpretation: at $>$90\\% greedy F1, residual errors are idiosyncratic and LP cannot rank corrections.\n\n\\subsection{Entity-Token LP Decomposition}\\label{app:entity_token_lp}\n\nFull-sequence LP averages over all output tokens, diluting entity-relevant signal with formatting and schema tokens.\nEntity-token LP restricts the average to tokens within entity text and type spans, yielding $4{\\times}$ higher within-instance variance (median 0.000644 vs.\\ 0.000161).\nTable~\\ref{tab:entity_token_lp} compares the two aggregation strategies on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Full-sequence vs.\\ entity-token LP on SciERC NER (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Entity-token LP has higher variance and stronger correlation, yet selection degrades.}\n\\label{tab:entity_token_lp}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nAggregation & Med.\\ var. & $\\rho$ & AUROC & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull-sequence & 0.000161 & 0.297 & 0.642 & $+$0.35 \\\\\nEntity text+type & 0.000644 & 0.440 & 0.719 & $-$0.73 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

1500	Despite stronger correlation ($+$48\%) and higher AUROC, entity-token LP produces worse selection, reinforcing that compression operates at the representation level rather than token aggregation: the correlation-selection gap persists even with a more targeted signal.
1501	
1502	\subsection{Entity-Level Majority Voting}\label{app:entity_merge}
1503	
1504	Entity-level majority voting constructs a merged output by including all (text, type) entity tuples that appear in at least $k$ of $N$ samples (with $k = N/2$ as the default majority threshold).
1505	Unlike best-of-$N$ selection (which picks one complete sample), merge combines evidence across samples at entity granularity.
1506	We sweep $k \in \{2, 3, \ldots, 8\}$ on each dataset (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$) and report the best threshold per dataset.
1507	
1508	\begin{table}[h]
1509	\centering
1510	\small

Only SciERC shows positive recovery; even with oracle $k$, CoNLL and Few-NERD degrade.}

```text
1	docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex:7:All target discrete-answer reasoning; structured prediction requires different gating signals because degeneracy operates at the entity-set level.
2	docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex:10:\citet{yao2026consistency} show consistency correlates with accuracy for LLM-based NER/RE; we show correlation alone does not ensure selection gains, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as blocking mechanisms.
3	docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex:21:Our selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, deguchi2024centroid}; unlike MT where MBR yields consistent gains \cite{freitag2022high} and code generation where execution-based MBR improves selection \cite{shi2022mbr}, instance degeneracy eliminates the headroom MBR needs even when utility is well-aligned ($\rho{=}0.52$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
4	docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex:22:\citet{eikema2025structured} find MBR utilities degrade on structured outputs; our diagnosis is complementary: degeneracy, not utility misalignment, is the primary bottleneck.
5	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:8:Unlike \citet{yao2026consistency}, who demonstrate that self-consistency correlates with task accuracy, we provide the first systematic empirical characterization of \textit{when and why} LP-based selection succeeds or fails for structured prediction, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as the two blocking mechanisms.
6	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:13:For selection, LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) in settings where both low instance degeneracy and adequate alignment are present, with gains scaling with $N$.
7	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:15:Three factors qualitatively shape this gap: (a)~\textit{instance degeneracy} (13\textendash 87\% across our benchmarks) compresses the candidate space; (b)~\textit{oracle headroom} bounds the achievable gain; (c)~\textit{LP-quality alignment} (within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$) determines whether LP can discriminate candidates in non-degenerate instances (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}).
8	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:16:Our diagnosis is complementary to minimum Bayes risk (MBR) utility misalignment \cite{eikema2025structured}: where MBR failures in structured prediction stem from poorly chosen utility functions, we find that degeneracy eliminates candidate diversity before utility alignment matters.
9	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:17:Within non-degenerate instances, MBR utility correlates positively with F1 ($\rho{=}0.52$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
10	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:18:Both mechanisms intensify with model capability: degeneracy rises with scale and training convergence (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). This creates a tension between model quality and test-time improvability.
11	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:23:[Omitted long matching line]
12	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:27:We evaluate five signals from three families (structural, surface, and model-internal) on SciERC \cite{luan2018scierc} as a primary medium-difficulty test bed, with Few-NERD \cite{ding2021fewnerd} (low-degeneracy contrast), CoNLL-2003 \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} (ceiling-effect control), and WNUT-17 \cite{derczynski2017wnut} (signal-collapse boundary), using two 8B models (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) with a 4B ablation (Qwen3-4B).
13	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:38:    Instance degeneracy causally modulates LP within fixed settings (epoch and temperature ablations), but does not predict cross-dataset direction ($\rho{=}0.052$, 23 configurations); structural signals fail by construction; supervised combinations and hidden state probes (Appendix~\ref{app:hidden_probe}) confirm the gap persists.
14	docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:41:    The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level aggregation, adapting entity voting from ensemble NER \cite{florian2003named} to stochastic sampling: LP-weighted construction yields $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC (multi-seed mean, $p{<}0.005$; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}).
15	docs/paper/sections/method.tex:61:We define \textbf{instance degeneracy} as the fraction of instances where all $N$ samples yield identical F1;\footnote{``Identical F1'' means constant F1 across all $N$ samples for a given instance, not necessarily F1${=}1.0$. Samples may differ in surface form but produce the same entity set and thus the same score.} for these instances, no signal can improve selection.
16	docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex:6:We identify three qualitative factors: instance degeneracy, oracle headroom, and LP-quality alignment; the gap persists at 72B zero-shot scale, confirming it is a structural property of the task rather than a fine-tuning artifact.
17	docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex:7:The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level \textit{construction}, adapting entity voting to stochastic sampling: LP-weighted aggregation improves F1 by $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC (multi-seed mean, $p{<}0.005$). Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.
18	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:10:\textbf{Few-NERD} \cite{ding2021fewnerd} (INTRA coarse; 37{,}648 instances, 8 entity types, ${\approx}$75\% F1) provides a low-degeneracy contrast where LP selection succeeds (\S\ref{sec:fewnerd}).
19	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:57:LP selection effectiveness is primarily determined by LP-quality alignment rather than degeneracy level (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
20	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:97:On SciERC ($n{=}529$, 2-seed sign tests\footnote{MDE${\approx}$1.5~pp at 80\% power ($n{=}529$); Few-NERD ($n{=}32{,}565$; MDE${\approx}$0.22~pp) provides the definitive test.}), the oracle (0.775) reveals 13.0~pp of headroom, but LP achieves $+0.2$~pp ($p{=}0.29$), below the MDE (${\approx}$1.5~pp); selection effects below this threshold cannot be reliably detected.
21	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:99:Few-NERD provides the definitive contrast: LP achieves $+1.39$~pp ($p{<}0.001$) at low degeneracy (\S\ref{sec:fewnerd}), while structural signals still degrade selection.

27	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:129:Few-NERD (Table~\ref{tab:fewnerd}) confirms LP selection at low degeneracy: $+1.39$~pp ($p{<}0.001$), while structural signals degrade selection (VC $-1.79$, SJ $-2.14$~pp). LP is 50$\times$ more seed-stable ($\sigma{=}0.04$ vs.\ SJ $2.02$~pp) and generalizes to LLaMA-3.1-8B (Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection}).
28	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:130:Training convergence modulates degeneracy: 5-epoch Few-NERD raises degeneracy to 31\% and halves LP gains (Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}).
29	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:133:Across 23 configurations, degeneracy does not predict LP gain ($\rho{=}0.052$, $p{=}0.81$; Figure~\ref{fig:degeneracy_scatter}), though within fixed settings it causally modulates LP ($\rho{=}{-}0.881$; Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_lp}).

34	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:170:Degeneracy scales with capability: 8B shows 17.8\% vs.\ 4B's 10.4\% on SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:degeneracy}).
35	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:173:LoRA rank${=}8$ (vs.\ 32) lowers degeneracy to 7.2\% but LP remains below greedy ($\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1}){\approx}0.24$); temperature manipulation ($T{=}1.2$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) confirms that low degeneracy and adequate alignment are independently necessary.

Majority vote also succeeds ($+1.39$\,pp); LP-weighting adds $+0.14$\,pp. Construction fails on Few-NERD 72B ZS ($-0.20$\,pp) and CoNLL ($-0.37$\,pp), where degeneracy exceeds $46$\%---consistent with the degeneracy-gating pattern in the fine-tuned regime ($n{=}3$ datasets).

48	docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:244:Construction and LP selection are complementary: construction dominates on SciERC ($+1.02$\,pp vs.\ LP $+0.04{\pm}0.48$\,pp, n.s.; $\rho{\approx}0.2$), while on Few-NERD both yield comparable gains ($+1.40$ vs.\ $+1.39$\,pp; $\rho{\approx}0.5$--$0.7$).
