50	docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:3:[Omitted long matching line]
51	docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:4:Three factors qualitatively constrain selection: instance degeneracy (17.8--67.9\%) compresses the candidate space, limited oracle headroom bounds achievable gains, and LP compression (within-instance $\rho$ 0.043--0.667) prevents exploiting the remainder; within non-degenerate instances, MBR utility correlates positively with F1 (SJ median $\rho{=}0.524$) \cite{eikema2022sampling}.
52	docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:6:Temperature disentangles degeneracy from alignment: at $T{=}1.2$, degeneracy drops to 14.9\% yet LP does not improve (median $\rho{=}0.225$; Table~\ref{tab:t_degeneracy}); the selection window narrows as models improve \cite{wang2023selfconsistency, snell2025scaling}.
53	docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:9:Entity-level construction bypasses both failure mechanisms (Table~\ref{tab:entity_construction}), complementary with LP selection and governed by LP-quality alignment (\S\ref{sec:prescriptive}). Construction generalizes to zero-shot 72B where degeneracy is low (SciERC: $+1.53$\,pp, recovering $10.2$\% of headroom; FT SciERC: $7.8$\%; FT Few-NERD: $11.3$\%), but fails at high degeneracy (${\geq}46$\%).

Counts are from the primary seed (seed 42); text references use 3-seed mean degeneracy rates.}
65	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:830:Despite MBR's success in machine translation \cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here: despite positive within-instance utility--F1 correlation, selection gains are limited by instance degeneracy, the core of the correlation-selection gap.
66	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1038:Table~\ref{tab:n_scaling_selection} traces the effect of sample count on degeneracy, oracle headroom, and per-signal selection gain.
67	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1040:LP selection improves monotonically with $N$ ($+0.39$~pp at $N{=}8$ to $+0.95$~pp at $N{=}32$), but even at $N{=}32$, where degeneracy falls to 4\% and oracle headroom reaches ${\sim}20$~pp, the best signal captures less than 5\% of the available improvement.
68	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1041:Degeneracy drops monotonically (43\% at $N{=}2$ to 4\% at $N{=}32$), yet reduced degeneracy does not unlock selection, consistent with the LoRA-rank ablation finding (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}).

72	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1119:Both quantities are strictly monotonic in $T$: degeneracy falls from 41.4\% ($T{=}0.5$) to 14.9\% ($T{=}1.2$), while LP range increases from 0.015 to 0.058 nats.
73	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1120:Temperature thus provides a direct lever for practitioners to shift the degeneracy regime.
74	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1121:However, even at $T{=}1.2$ where degeneracy (14.9\%) approaches Few-NERD levels (${\sim}$13\%), within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$ remains at 0.225, far below Few-NERD's ${\sim}$0.5.
75	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1122:This confirms that temperature controls degeneracy but cannot improve LP-quality alignment, which is largely determined by task structure and remains relatively stable across generation configurations (\S\ref{app:epoch_confound}). LP-quality alignment, not degeneracy, is the primary predictor of LP selection direction (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).

82	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1274:(4)~Low degeneracy (13.0\%) combined with strong LP-quality alignment ($\rho{\approx}0.5$--$0.7$) enables LP selection: candidates differ in F1 more often, and LP can rank them effectively. 83	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1278:Table~\ref{tab:per_type_lp} stratifies LP selection by entity type on Few-NERD, sorted by degeneracy rate.
84	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1279:The degeneracy-gating mechanism holds at entity-type granularity: 7 of 8 types show positive LP selection gains, and the sole negative type (Art, $-0.17$~pp) has the highest degeneracy (19.8\%).
85	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1280:The correlation between per-type degeneracy and oracle headroom is strong and significant (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.881$, $p{=}0.004$; exact permutation $p{=}0.005$); degeneracy directly constrains the headroom available for selection.
86	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1287:\caption{Per-entity-type LP selection on Few-NERD INTRA coarse (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), sorted by degeneracy. LP $\Delta$: LP selection F1 minus greedy. 7/8 types show positive $\Delta$; Art (highest degeneracy) is the sole exception.}
87	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1308:\caption{CoNLL-2003 per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}16$, $T{=}1.0$). All types exceed 20\% degeneracy and show negative LP selection, consistent with the dataset's ceiling-regime performance (F1 $>$ 90\%).}
88	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1325:\caption{SciERC per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Within-dataset, degeneracy alone does not predict LP effectiveness (Spearman $\rho{=}0.14$, $p{=}0.79$); additional type-specific factors likely modulate selection at this granularity.}
89	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1341:The cross-dataset degeneracy gradient (61.7\% $\to$ 17.8\% $\to$ 13.0\%) is inversely associated with LP selection effectiveness at the macro level ($n{=}3$ datasets; exploratory).\footnote{Partial Spearman correlation controlling for entity-type frequency yields $\rho{=}{-}0.47$ (vs.\ raw $\rho{=}{-}0.38$); type frequency does not confound the degeneracy--LP relationship.}
90	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1342:At finer per-type granularity, selection depends on the interaction of degeneracy, oracle headroom, and type-specific LP-quality alignment.
91	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1344:Low degeneracy alone does not ensure selection gains: CoNLL MISC (23\%, headroom 12.6~pp) and SciERC OtherScientificTerm (6.2\%, headroom 12.5~pp) both show negative LP selection despite available headroom; LP correlation strength with the type-specific quality function is an additional prerequisite.
92	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1353:Platt scaling substantially improves probability calibration (ECE drops from 0.26--0.39 to 0.01--0.08), but better-calibrated probabilities do not translate to better selection.
93	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1376:\paragraph{Training convergence modulates degeneracy.}
94	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1378:Extended training increases greedy F1 by $+2.95$~pp (0.748 $\to$ 0.777) but nearly triples instance degeneracy (11.1\% $\to$ 31.3\%), reducing the fraction of instances with non-degenerate outputs from 85.6\% to 68.7\%.
95	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1379:LP selection gains drop correspondingly ($+1.37$~pp $\to$ $+0.62$~pp), consistent with the degeneracy-gating mechanism identified in the main text (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
96	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1383:Standard early stopping practice thus naturally selects the low-degeneracy regime where LP selection is most effective.
97	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1384:This finding refines the narrative: degeneracy is not a fixed property of datasets but is modulated by the degree of training convergence. Over-trained models compress their output distributions, collapsing diversity even on datasets that support it under well-regularized training.
98	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1389:[Omitted long matching line]

100	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1407:Degeneracy increases monotonically (6.4\% $\to$ 17.8\% $\to$ 47.8\%), and within-instance LP-quality correlation degrades in tandem (median $\rho$: 0.319 $\to$ 0.262 $\to$ 0.112; mean $\rho$: 0.273 $\to$ 0.179 $\to$ 0.066), confirming that overtraining erodes both diversity and LP alignment simultaneously.
101	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1409:LP selection does not yield significant gains at any training duration ($-1.24$, $+0.64$, $+1.02$~pp; all below the ${\approx}$1.5~pp MDE), despite oracle headroom ranging from 8.4 to 15.0~pp.
102	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1410:The 3-epoch model has higher within-instance alignment ($\rho{=}0.319$) than the 5-epoch model ($\rho{=}0.262$), yet LP selection is worse ($-1.24$ vs.\ $+0.64$~pp): at 6.4\% degeneracy, more non-degenerate instances enter the candidate pool, but LP ranking precision is too low to exploit them, so the broader pool hurts rather than helps. This illustrates that low degeneracy does not ensure selection gains; LP-quality alignment determines selection direction.
103	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1411:At 10 epochs, the model retains competitive greedy F1 (0.649 vs.\ 0.650 at 3 epochs) yet conditions are unfavorable: 47.8\% degenerate instances and $\rho{=}0.112$.

108	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1435:The Few-NERD epoch analysis covers 2 points (3 vs.\ 5 epochs); the SciERC curve provides a 3-point gradient showing monotonic degeneracy increase with simultaneous within-instance alignment degradation.
109	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1436:CoNLL epoch variation remains unverified; high-degeneracy datasets (CoNLL at 59--89\%) may exhibit a ceiling effect where additional training has limited marginal impact.
110	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1442:The non-tied subset analysis (Table~\ref{tab:nontied_main}) identifies within-instance LP range (max minus min LP across $N$ samples) as a per-instance degeneracy proxy: instances with near-zero LP range carry no discriminative signal.
111	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1445:DALS improves over pure LP selection when degeneracy is moderate, provides partial damage control when LP selection is harmful, and defaults to greedy when LP range signals no information.
112	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1446:While gains are modest and seed-sensitive, LP range serves as an operationally accessible degeneracy proxy, though the mixed cross-dataset results confirm that degeneracy gating alone cannot ensure positive selection outcomes.
113	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1452:This sensitivity reinforces the diagnostic framing of DALS: per-dataset $\tau$ tuning is required for positive gains, and even optimally tuned DALS cannot overcome weak LP-quality alignment on high-degeneracy datasets.
114	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1456:To test whether LP-quality alignment drives selection gains within datasets, we restrict LP selection to the favorable subset: non-degenerate instances whose within-instance LP range exceeds the dataset-specific median (SciERC: 0.040 nats, CoNLL: 0.062, Few-NERD: 0.067).
115	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1462:\caption{LP selection on the favorable subset (non-degenerate instances with LP range above the dataset-specific median) per dataset (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). CI: bootstrap 95\% confidence interval.}
116	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1475:On SciERC, restricting to the favorable subset recovers a significant LP gain, consistent with LP-quality alignment driving selection within the non-degenerate pool.
117	docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1524:On degenerate subsets (instances where all $N$ samples yield identical F1), merge is uniformly negative across all three datasets, confirming that entity-level aggregation cannot bypass the diversity bottleneck identified in the main text (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).

Degen.: instance degeneracy (\\%).}\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:195:SciERC & 8B FT  & .644 & 13.0 & 17.8 & .379 & .360 & .267 & .205 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:198:Few-NERD & 8B FT & .748 & 12.4 & 13.0 & --- & --- & --- & .220 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:199:         & 72B ZS & .628 & --- & 46.2 & .416 & .345 & --- & .119 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:201:CoNLL  & 8B FT  & .908 & ${\\approx}$2 & 61.7 & .424 & .436 & .400 & .302 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:202:       & 72B ZS & .712 & 5.3 & 67.7 & .297 & .304 & .302 & $-$.002 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:207:LP $\\rho$ drops sharply without fine-tuning across all three datasets (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_comparison}), and LP selection fails on all. Structural signals generalize to zero-shot (Few-NERD VC $\\rho{=}0.416$, SJ $0.345$). Degeneracy increases (Few-NERD: $46.2$\\% vs.\\ $13.0$\\%; CoNLL: $67.7$\\% vs.\\ $61.7$\\%).\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:209:LP-weighted construction yields $+1.53$\\,pp on SciERC 72B ZS ($p{=}0.008$, CI [$+0.25$, $+2.82$]; $d{=}0.10$), recovering $10.2$\\% of headroom (FT: $7.8$\\% SciERC, $11.3$\\% Few-NERD). Majority vote also succeeds ($+1.39$\\,pp); LP-weighting adds $+0.14$\\,pp. Construction fails on Few-NERD 72B ZS ($-0.20$\\,pp) and CoNLL ($-0.37$\\,pp), where degeneracy exceeds $46$\\%---consistent with the degeneracy-gating pattern in the fine-tuned regime ($n{=}3$ datasets).\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:230:SciERC & LP & $.646{\\pm}.002$ & $\\mathbf{.656{\\pm}.003}$ & $+1.02{\\pm}0.49$\\,pp & .004 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:233:Few-NERD & LP & $.748$ & $\\mathbf{.762{\\pm}.005}$ & $+1.40{\\pm}0.51$\\,pp & ${<}.001$ \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:236:CoNLL$^\\ddagger$ & Unif. & .908 & .904 & $-0.39$\\,pp & n.s. \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:241:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:244:Construction and LP selection are complementary: construction dominates on SciERC ($+1.02$\\,pp vs.\\ LP $+0.04{\\pm}0.48$\\,pp, n.s.; $\\rho{\\approx}0.2$), while on Few-NERD both yield comparable gains ($+1.40$ vs.\\ $+1.39$\\,pp; $\\rho{\\approx}0.5$--$0.7$).\ndocs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:247:This routes Few-NERD to LP selection ($+1.39$\\,pp); SciERC, CoNLL, and WNUT-17 fall below $\\tau$ and route to greedy. The conservative protocol trades SciERC's missed construction gain ($+1.02$\\,pp) for protection against negative outcomes (CoNLL $-0.39$\\,pp, WNUT-17 n.s.).\ndocs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:3:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:4:Three factors qualitatively constrain selection: instance degeneracy (17.8--67.9\\%) compresses the candidate space, limited oracle headroom bounds achievable gains, and LP compression (within-instance $\\rho$ 0.043--0.667) prevents exploiting the remainder; within non-degenerate instances, MBR utility correlates positively with F1 (SJ median $\\rho{=}0.524$) \\cite{eikema2022sampling}.\ndocs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:6:Temperature disentangles degeneracy from alignment: at $T{=}1.2$, degeneracy drops to 14.9\\% yet LP does not improve (median $\\rho{=}0.225$; Table~\\ref{tab:t_degeneracy}); the selection window narrows as models improve \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, snell2025scaling}.\ndocs/paper/sections/discussion.tex:9:Entity-level construction bypasses both failure mechanisms (Table~\\ref{tab:entity_construction}), complementary with LP selection and governed by LP-quality alignment (\\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}). Construction generalizes to zero-shot 72B where degeneracy is low (SciERC: $+1.53$\\,pp, recovering $10.2$\\% of headroom; FT SciERC: $7.8$\\%; FT Few-NERD: $11.3$\\%), but fails at high degeneracy (${\\geq}46$\\%).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:231:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:350:\\paragraph{Instance degeneracy statistics.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:351:Table~\\ref{tab:degeneracy} quantifies instance degeneracy across configurations. ``Constant F1'' denotes instances where all $N$ samples receive identical instance-level F1; ``Constant utility'' denotes instances where MBR (SJ) utility is identical across candidates, preventing ranking.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:356:\\caption{Instance degeneracy statistics ($T{=}1.0$). ``Constant F1'': all $N$ samples receive identical instance-level F1. ``Constant utility'': MBR (SJ) utility identical across candidates. 4B data confirms degeneracy scales with model capability.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:357:\\label{tab:degeneracy}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:362:Constant F1 & 17.8\\% & 10.4\\% & 61.7\\% \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:424:(c)~Ensemble or mixture-of-experts settings generate structurally diverse candidates by design, reducing instance degeneracy below the levels observed in our single-model sampling.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:685:Table~\\ref{tab:within_rho} reports median within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$ across datasets, computed over non-degenerate instances (those with ${\\geq}2$ distinct F1 values across $N{=}8$ samples).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:687:CoNLL alignment is moderate ($\\rho{\\approx}0.51$), but the valid-instance fraction is low (38\\% for Qwen, 11\\% for LLaMA) because degeneracy eliminates most headroom before alignment can operate.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:688:LP-quality alignment is the primary predictor of selection direction; degeneracy constrains headroom but does not independently predict whether LP selection gains materialize (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:693:\\caption{Within-instance LP-quality alignment across datasets ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). $\\rho$: median Spearman correlation between LP and F1 within each instance, over non-degenerate instances ($n_{\\text{valid}}$: ${\\geq}2$ distinct F1 values). Counts are from the primary seed (seed 42); text references use 3-seed mean degeneracy rates.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:830:Despite MBR's success in machine translation \\cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \\cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here: despite positive within-instance utility--F1 correlation, selection gains are limited by instance degeneracy, the core of the correlation-selection gap.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1038:Table~\\ref{tab:n_scaling_selection} traces the effect of sample count on degeneracy, oracle headroom, and per-signal selection gain.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1040:LP selection improves monotonically with $N$ ($+0.39$~pp at $N{=}8$ to $+0.95$~pp at $N{=}32$), but even at $N{=}32$, where degeneracy falls to 4\\% and oracle headroom reaches ${\\sim}20$~pp, the best signal captures less than 5\\% of the available improvement.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1041:Degeneracy drops monotonically (43\\% at $N{=}2$ to 4\\% at $N{=}32$), yet reduced degeneracy does not unlock selection, consistent with the LoRA-rank ablation finding (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_main}).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1104:1.0 & $+$13.0 & 0.360 & 0.205 & ${\\approx}$0.39 & ${\\approx}$0.612 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1117:\\paragraph{Temperature controls degeneracy and LP spread.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1118:Table~\\ref{tab:t_degeneracy} reports instance degeneracy (constant F1, gold-filtered) and median within-instance LP range across four temperatures on SciERC.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1119:Both quantities are strictly monotonic in $T$: degeneracy falls from 41.4\\% ($T{=}0.5$) to 14.9\\% ($T{=}1.2$), while LP range increases from 0.015 to 0.058 nats.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1120:Temperature thus provides a direct lever for practitioners to shift the degeneracy regime.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1121:However, even at $T{=}1.2$ where degeneracy (14.9\\%) approaches Few-NERD levels (${\\sim}$13\\%), within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$ remains at 0.225, far below Few-NERD's ${\\sim}$0.5.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1122:This confirms that temperature controls degeneracy but cannot improve LP-quality alignment, which is largely determined by task structure and remains relatively stable across generation configurations (\\S\\ref{app:epoch_confound}). LP-quality alignment, not degeneracy, is the primary predictor of LP selection direction (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1127:\\caption{Temperature sensitivity of degeneracy and LP spread (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$). Degeneracy: constant-F1, gold-filtered. LP range: median within-instance range.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1128:\\label{tab:t_degeneracy}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1135:1.0 & 17.8 & 0.042 & 0.405 & 0.700 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1248:Instance degeneracy is 13.0\\% (vs.\\ 17.8\\% on SciERC, 61.7\\% on CoNLL).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1259:LP & .220 & .578 & $\\mathbf{+1.39}$\\,pp & [+1.18, +1.53] \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1271:(1)~All five signals achieve positive QE ($\\rho$ 0.22--0.39); quality estimation works on Few-NERD. VC leads ($\\rho{=}0.39$), followed by FK ($0.31$), SJ ($0.30$), EM ($0.29$), LP ($0.22$); the ranking matches SciERC where structural signals also outperform LP for quality estimation.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1274:(4)~Low degeneracy (13.0\\%) combined with strong LP-quality alignment ($\\rho{\\approx}0.5$--$0.7$) enables LP selection: candidates differ in F1 more often, and LP can rank them effectively. This contrasts with SciERC (17.8\\% degeneracy but weak alignment $\\rho{\\approx}0.24$, LP $+0.61$~pp) and CoNLL (61.7\\%, LP $-0.85$~pp).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1278:Table~\\ref{tab:per_type_lp} stratifies LP selection by entity type on Few-NERD, sorted by degeneracy rate.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1279:The degeneracy-gating mechanism holds at entity-type granularity: 7 of 8 types show positive LP selection gains, and the sole negative type (Art, $-0.17$~pp) has the highest degeneracy (19.8\\%).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1280:The correlation between per-type degeneracy and oracle headroom is strong and significant (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.881$, $p{=}0.004$; exact permutation $p{=}0.005$); degeneracy directly constrains the headroom available for selection.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1287:\\caption{Per-entity-type LP selection on Few-NERD INTRA coarse (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), sorted by degeneracy. LP $\\Delta$: LP selection F1 minus greedy. 7/8 types show positive $\\Delta$; Art (highest degeneracy) is the sole exception.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1308:\\caption{CoNLL-2003 per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}16$, $T{=}1.0$). All types exceed 20\\% degeneracy and show negative LP selection, consistent with the dataset's ceiling-regime performance (F1 $>$ 90\\%).}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1325:\\caption{SciERC per-type LP selection (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Within-dataset, degeneracy alone does not predict LP effectiveness (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.14$, $p{=}0.79$); additional type-specific factors likely modulate selection at this granularity.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1341:The cross-dataset degeneracy gradient (61.7\\% $\\to$ 17.8\\% $\\to$ 13.0\\%) is inversely associated with LP selection effectiveness at the macro level ($n{=}3$ datasets; exploratory).\\footnote{Partial Spearman correlation controlling for entity-type frequency yields $\\rho{=}{-}0.47$ (vs.\\ raw $\\rho{=}{-}0.38$); type frequency does not confound the degeneracy--LP relationship.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1342:At finer per-type granularity, selection depends on the interaction of degeneracy, oracle headroom, and type-specific LP-quality alignment.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1344:Low degeneracy alone does not ensure selection gains: CoNLL MISC (23\\%, headroom 12.6~pp) and SciERC OtherScientificTerm (6.2\\%, headroom 12.5~pp) both show negative LP selection despite available headroom; LP correlation strength with the type-specific quality function is an additional prerequisite.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1353:Platt scaling substantially improves probability calibration (ECE drops from 0.26--0.39 to 0.01--0.08), but better-calibrated probabilities do not translate to better selection.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1376:\\paragraph{Training convergence modulates degeneracy.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1378:Extended training increases greedy F1 by $+2.95$~pp (0.748 $\\to$ 0.777) but nearly triples instance degeneracy (11.1\\% $\\to$ 31.3\\%), reducing the fraction of instances with non-degenerate outputs from 85.6\\% to 68.7\\%.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1379:LP selection gains drop correspondingly ($+1.37$~pp $\\to$ $+0.62$~pp), consistent with the degeneracy-gating mechanism identified in the main text (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1383:Standard early stopping practice thus naturally selects the low-degeneracy regime where LP selection is most effective.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1384:This finding refines the narrative: degeneracy is not a fixed property of datasets but is modulated by the degree of training convergence. Over-trained models compress their output distributions, collapsing diversity even on datasets that support it under well-regularized training.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1389:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1396:Instance degen.\\ (\\%) & 11.1 & 31.3 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1407:Degeneracy increases monotonically (6.4\\% $\\to$ 17.8\\% $\\to$ 47.8\\%), and within-instance LP-quality correlation degrades in tandem (median $\\rho$: 0.319 $\\to$ 0.262 $\\to$ 0.112; mean $\\rho$: 0.273 $\\to$ 0.179 $\\to$ 0.066), confirming that overtraining erodes both diversity and LP alignment simultaneously.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1409:LP selection does not yield significant gains at any training duration ($-1.24$, $+0.64$, $+1.02$~pp; all below the ${\\approx}$1.5~pp MDE), despite oracle headroom ranging from 8.4 to 15.0~pp.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1410:The 3-epoch model has higher within-instance alignment ($\\rho{=}0.319$) than the 5-epoch model ($\\rho{=}0.262$), yet LP selection is worse ($-1.24$ vs.\\ $+0.64$~pp): at 6.4\\% degeneracy, more non-degenerate instances enter the candidate pool, but LP ranking precision is too low to exploit them, so the broader pool hurts rather than helps. This illustrates that low degeneracy does not ensure selection gains; LP-quality alignment determines selection direction.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1411:At 10 epochs, the model retains competitive greedy F1 (0.649 vs.\\ 0.650 at 3 epochs) yet conditions are unfavorable: 47.8\\% degenerate instances and $\\rho{=}0.112$.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1412:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1418:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1425:Instance degen.\\ (\\%) & 6.4 & 17.8 & 47.8 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1426:LP $\\Delta$ (pp) & $-$1.24 & $+$0.64 & $+$1.02 \\\\\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1435:The Few-NERD epoch analysis covers 2 points (3 vs.\\ 5 epochs); the SciERC curve provides a 3-point gradient showing monotonic degeneracy increase with simultaneous within-instance alignment degradation.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1436:CoNLL epoch variation remains unverified; high-degeneracy datasets (CoNLL at 59--89\\%) may exhibit a ceiling effect where additional training has limited marginal impact.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1442:The non-tied subset analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:nontied_main}) identifies within-instance LP range (max minus min LP across $N$ samples) as a per-instance degeneracy proxy: instances with near-zero LP range carry no discriminative signal.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1445:DALS improves over pure LP selection when degeneracy is moderate, provides partial damage control when LP selection is harmful, and defaults to greedy when LP range signals no information.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1446:While gains are modest and seed-sensitive, LP range serves as an operationally accessible degeneracy proxy, though the mixed cross-dataset results confirm that degeneracy gating alone cannot ensure positive selection outcomes.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1452:This sensitivity reinforces the diagnostic framing of DALS: per-dataset $\\tau$ tuning is required for positive gains, and even optimally tuned DALS cannot overcome weak LP-quality alignment on high-degeneracy datasets.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1456:To test whether LP-quality alignment drives selection gains within datasets, we restrict LP selection to the favorable subset: non-degenerate instances whose within-instance LP range exceeds the dataset-specific median (SciERC: 0.040 nats, CoNLL: 0.062, Few-NERD: 0.067).\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1462:\\caption{LP selection on the favorable subset (non-degenerate instances with LP range above the dataset-specific median) per dataset (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). CI: bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval.}\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1475:On SciERC, restricting to the favorable subset recovers a significant LP gain, consistent with LP-quality alignment driving selection within the non-degenerate pool.\ndocs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:1524:On degenerate subsets (instances where all $N$ samples yield identical F1), merge is uniformly negative across all three datasets, confirming that entity-level aggregation cannot bypass the diversity bottleneck identified in the main text (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Five confidence signals achieve reliable NER quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82), enabling selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\,pp F1 at 80\% coverage). Instance-level selection fails due to degeneracy and LP compression---a structural property that persists at 72B zero-shot scale. Entity-level construction yields $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC ($p{<}0.005$), generalizing to 72B zero-shot where degeneracy is low ($+1.53$\,pp on SciERC). For practitioners: use QE for selective prediction; apply construction when multi-sample generation is feasible; assess LP-alignment to route between strategies. As models improve, degeneracy rises and the selection window narrows; content-aware verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers} may be needed to close the remaining gap.
4	
```

### Tool result

_source line 60 · 2026-05-19T18:18:34.115Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Fi345qUZhAsV8zSnM31rfk`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Self-consistency yields large gains on discrete-answer tasks, but named entity recognition (NER) produces \textit{sets} of entities with partial overlap, where no majority vote analog exists.
3	We evaluate five confidence signals for quality estimation (QE) and sample selection on four NER benchmarks across fine-tuned 4B\textendash 8B and zero-shot 72B models.
4	All signals achieve reliable QE ($\rho$ up to 0.45 on CoNLL-2003, area under the ROC curve [AUROC] up to 0.82), enabling selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\,pp F1 at 80\% coverage).
5	For instance-level selection, log-probability (LP) succeeds only where LP-quality alignment is strong, and gains are absent elsewhere despite large oracle headroom (the \textbf{correlation-selection gap}).
6	We identify three qualitative factors: instance degeneracy, oracle headroom, and LP-quality alignment; the gap persists at 72B zero-shot scale, confirming it is a structural property of the task rather than a fine-tuning artifact.
7	The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level \textit{construction}, adapting entity voting to stochastic sampling: LP-weighted aggregation improves F1 by $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC (multi-seed mean, $p{<}0.005$). Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XpV4yiuUtAWd8jTXLSUBjP`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related}
2	
3	\paragraph{Self-consistency and test-time scaling.}
4	Self-consistency \cite{wang2023selfconsistency} improves reasoning via majority voting; extensions handle free-form text \cite{chen2024usc, thirukovalluru2024atomic, manakul2023selfcheckgpt}.
5	Test-time scaling studies \cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys} and reward-free selectors \cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty, wang2025rankedvoting, oh2026latentsc} rely on answer-level agreement.
6	Adaptive variants gate on answer agreement \cite{aggarwal2023adaptive}, input difficulty \cite{wang2025make, feng2025optimal}, or response confidence \cite{kim2026reasc}; \citet{chen2025twofailures} diagnose SC failure modes without proposing gating.
7	All target discrete-answer reasoning; structured prediction requires different gating signals because degeneracy operates at the entity-set level.
8	
9	\paragraph{Consistency for structured IE.}
10	\citet{yao2026consistency} show consistency correlates with accuracy for LLM-based NER/RE; we show correlation alone does not ensure selection gains, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as blocking mechanisms.
11	CoCoA \cite{vashurin2025cocoa} fuses confidence with consistency; our supervised baseline confirms combination is not the bottleneck (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}).
12	Earlier work on confidence for structured prediction \cite{culotta2004confidence, mejer2010confidence, fomicheva2020unsupervised} establishes the paradigm we extend to set-valued outputs.
13	Ensemble approaches aggregate predictions from multiple models or prompts \cite{florian2003named, xiao2025el4ner}; \citet{xie2023zeroshot} propose two-stage entity voting for zero-shot NER, the closest prior work to our entity-level construction.
14	LLM-Blender \cite{jiang2023llmblender} fuses outputs from multiple LLMs via pairwise ranking, whereas our single-model stochastic sampling derives diversity from temperature rather than model heterogeneity.
15	Our entity-level construction aggregates across samples from a single fine-tuned model, without requiring model diversity or prompt engineering.
16	Our evaluation builds on generative IE \cite{zhou2024uniner, sainz2024gollie, li2023codeie, wadhwa2023revisiting, xie2024selfimproving} and constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided, dong2024xgrammar, park2024grammar, schall2025hidden}.
17	\citet{yun2025price} show structured formats induce diversity collapse; our LP compression (median range 0.041 nats; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) corroborates this for NER.
18	
19	\paragraph{Uncertainty estimation and MBR decoding.}
20	Surveys cover confidence estimation \cite{geng2024confidence}, uncertainty quantification \cite{xia2025survey}, and calibration \cite{hobelsberger2025systematic}; semantic uncertainty \cite{kuhn2023semantic, farquhar2024detecting} and P(True) \cite{kadavath2022language} provide model-internal alternatives.
21	Our selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, deguchi2024centroid}; unlike MT where MBR yields consistent gains \cite{freitag2022high} and code generation where execution-based MBR improves selection \cite{shi2022mbr}, instance degeneracy eliminates the headroom MBR needs even when utility is well-aligned ($\rho{=}0.52$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
22	\citet{eikema2025structured} find MBR utilities degrade on structured outputs; our diagnosis is complementary: degeneracy, not utility misalignment, is the primary bottleneck.
23	Our QE results connect to selective prediction \cite{geifman2017selective, kuleshov2015calibrated, varshney2022investigating}: signals with AUROC up to 0.82 support abstention policies independent of sample selection.
24	
25	\paragraph{Best-of-$N$ with learned verifiers.}
26	\citet{gao2023scaling} establish BoN scaling laws and \citet{chen2021evaluating} define the pass@$k$ metric for code generation; our unsupervised signals face a more fundamental limitation than reward overoptimization.
27	Trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, lightman2024verify, fernandes2022quality} and self-evaluation guided search \cite{xie2023selfevaluation} learn per-sample scoring.
28	CISC \cite{taubenfeld2025cisc} and Self-Certainty \cite{kang2025selfcertainty} derive per-sample confidence from hidden states rather than output statistics, but require architecture-specific access to intermediate representations; these hidden-state signals remain untested for structured prediction (\S Limitations).
29	Fine-tuning \cite{kirk2024understanding} and constrained decoding \cite{holtzman2020curious, vijayakumar2018diverse} both limit the diversity recoverable for selection.
30	
```

</details>

```text
228	\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}
229	Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the selection analysis to five additional configurations.
230	On LLaMA SciERC, where output diversity remains substantial (18\% identical outputs, 39\% unique), LP achieves the highest selection F1 (0.652) at $N{=}8$ but still falls within bootstrap noise of single-sample (0.646), replicating the gap observed on Qwen SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:selection}).
231	On LLaMA FewNERD ($n{=}32{,}565$, 3-seed mean), LP is the only signal with positive selection ($+0.36$~pp), confirming LP as the most robust selection signal across model families (Qwen FewNERD: $+1.39$~pp, Table~\ref{tab:fewnerd}). All structural signals degrade selection (VC $-2.39$~pp, SJ $-0.52$~pp), consistent with the Qwen pattern. LLaMA FewNERD degeneracy ($23.4{\pm}4.7$\%, 3 seeds) is higher than Qwen ($12.7$\%), illustrating the capability-diversity tension: LLaMA achieves stronger greedy F1 ($0.784$ vs.\ $0.748$, $+3.6$~pp) but at the cost of higher degeneracy ($+10.7$~pp) and reduced oracle headroom ($10.0$ vs.\ $12.4$~pp).
232	On CoNLL, 48--87\% of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples; this reflects a ceiling effect (single-sample F1 ${\approx}$0.91--0.92) rather than signal failure, and selection analysis on these configurations is uninformative because there is little per-sample variance for any signal to exploit.
```

Now I have a very good picture. Let me also check the LLaMA FewNERD 12.7% reference. The text says "LLaMA FewNERD degeneracy ($23.4{\pm}4.7$\%, 3 seeds) is higher than Qwen ($12.7$\%)"

The canonical degen for FewNERD 8B FT 3-seed mean is 13.0%. But the text uses 12.7% for Qwen FewNERD degen. Let me check if this is a problem.


Looking at the tables more carefully, I can confirm the 13.0% value appears consistently across the FewNERD results in the main tables and appendix, so that's the canonical number I should be working with. I'm noticing a discrepancy in the seed values—the context lists four numbers (13.75/13.1/12.1/11.77) but claims it's a 3-seed mean of 13.0%, which doesn't add up. There's also a separate issue where the epoch confound section shows different degeneracy values (13.8% vs. 11.1%) depending on whether the full test set or a 5K subsample was used, which could explain some of the variation I'm seeing across the document. Looking at the seed values more carefully, the 3-seed mean of 13.75, 13.1, and 12.1 averages to about 13.0%, while including the fourth value of 11.77 brings it down to 12.7%. The task description notes that 12.7% was previously cited as an incorrect or outdated value for FewNERD degradation, so the appendix reference to 12.7% appears to be using the wrong metric when it should be 13.0%.

Now I'm checking consistency across other degradation metrics in the paper—WNUT-17 shows 67.9% in the table, the discussion mentions a range from 17.8% to 67.9%, and the introduction cites 13% to 87%, where the 87% appears to come from the LLaMA CoNLL analysis showing that nearly all instances produce identical outputs across samples.

For the construction deltas, I'm verifying that the reported improvements are consistent across all mentions: SciERC 8B fine-tuning shows +1.02 percentage points everywhere it appears, FewNERD shows +1.40 consistently, and I'm checking the CoNLL results as well. Now I'm verifying the signal ρ values from the quality estimation table — VC, SJ, and LP correlations for SciERC all check out against the intro. For EM on CoNLL Qwen, the text mentions EM leading in both ρ and AUROC with the value 0.816, but I need to confirm whether that's actually the ρ value or if it's referring to the AUROC instead.

Looking at the actual numbers, EM's ρ of .451 does beat out the other methods on CoNLL Qwen, so that checks out. The AUROC values are consistently rounded to 0.82 in the abstract and intro, which matches the table's maximum of .816. The LP improvement on FewNERD is consistently reported as +1.39 percentage points across the paper. Now I'm digging into the LLaMA FewNERD degeneracy claim — the appendix mentions 23.4±4.7% for LLaMA but I need to see what the full comparison is.

The appendix line 231 cuts off mid-sentence comparing LLaMA's degeneracy to Qwen's, and the context indicates that 12.7% was an older, incorrect value that got cited. The canonical value should be 13.0% based on the 8B FT 3-seed mean, but the appendix appears to use 12.7% in this specific comparison. This looks like a genuine inconsistency — either the appendix is using an outdated number or pulling from a different calculation. Let me check what the FewNERD table actually shows for the degeneracy value. So the 13.0% appears to be the 3-seed mean, while 13.8% is the full test set result and 12.7% is likely from a different seed or an older version that's been marked as incorrect. The canonical value seems to be 13.0%. Now I'm searching for other potentially incorrect values like the 48.4% CoNLL figure or the 49% N=16 value to see if there are similar discrepancies elsewhere.

docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:37:\caption{Signal robustness across configurations (Spearman $\rho$). Top: temperature on Qwen SciERC. Middle: sample size on Qwen SciERC. Bottom: transfer across model families (both 8B) (LLaMA CoNLL $N{=}16$, 3-seed mean $\pm\sigma$). $^\dagger$Mean over 3 seeds ($\sigma{=}0.013$; individual: 0.398, 0.423, 0.410). $\sigma$ computed as sample standard deviation.}
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:57:\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Across model families} (LLaMA CoNLL, $N{=}16$, 3 seeds)} \\
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:82:The magnitude of $N$-scaling gains is setting-dependent (LLaMA SciERC NER yields $+0.016$ and Qwen CoNLL NER $+0.014$), though the direction is consistently positive.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:83:LLaMA CoNLL exhibits the most uniform scaling: all four consistency signals gain $\Delta\rho{\approx}{+}0.042$ (range: $+0.039$ to $+0.044$) while LP gains only $+0.004$ (Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:84:FK $N$-scaling is seed-dependent: Qwen SciERC gains range from $+0.005$ to $+0.023$ across seeds (mean $+0.016$), while LLaMA CoNLL yields $+0.039$ (3-seed mean); individual seed FK shifts vary substantially under the unified pipeline, so this should not be interpreted as a robust trend.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:92:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:200:EM ranks first across all four tested conditional configurations: SciERC NER ($\rho{=}0.360$), CoNLL Qwen $N{=}16$ ($\rho{=}0.466$), CoNLL LLaMA 3-seed ($\rho{=}0.462$), and SciERC RE ($\rho{=}0.417$). This holds despite full-set rankings ranging from \#1 (CoNLL Qwen) to \#5 (SciERC RE).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:237:\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 (macro, instance-averaged) across configurations ($T{=}1.0$, 3-seed mean). Best non-oracle signal per row in \textbf{bold}. LP is the only signal with positive selection on both FewNERD configurations (Qwen $+1.39$~pp, Table~\ref{tab:fewnerd}; LLaMA $+0.36$~pp). At $N{=}16$ on LLaMA CoNLL, VC achieves small but significant gains ($+0.28$~pp, $p{<}0.03$; see text below). CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:249:LLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:256:For quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\rho$ improves by $+0.047$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\Delta\rho{\leq}0.021$).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:258:This asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\ LP $\rho{=}0.307$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\Delta{=}{+}0.163$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while LP does not.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:263:While quality estimation benefits sublinearly from $N$-scaling, selection behavior at $N{=}16$ reveals a nuanced exception on LLaMA CoNLL.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:335:LLaMA/CoNLL & 88\% & --- \\
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:409:On LLaMA CoNLL ($n{=}2{,}756$), all signals pass equivalence at every $\delta$ ($p{<}0.001$), consistent with the near-zero selection deltas ($|\Delta|{\leq}0.1$~pp) and tight CIs at this sample size.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:412:These external anchors confirm that the equivalence conclusion is configuration-dependent: LLaMA CoNLL selection is robustly equivalent to single-sample under any reasonable margin, while SciERC and Qwen CoNLL selection shows detectable degradation at externally calibrated thresholds.
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:552:[Omitted long matching line]

_source line 97 · 2026-05-19T18:22:29.982Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01R2F1PC8URD1fvpJFZJ4U6A`</summary>

```text
1	Our evaluation covers four NER datasets; an exploratory RE extension on SciERC is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension} but generalization to other RE benchmarks remains unvalidated.
2	All experiments use English-language datasets; cross-lingual generalization remains untested.
3	Entity-level construction assumes the model produces useful partial outputs across samples; when model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17, 51\% F1${=}0$), this precondition fails and construction cannot improve over greedy.
4	We cover 4B\textendash 8B fine-tuned and 72B zero-shot; the gap persists at 72B but behavior with closed-source APIs or 72B fine-tuning is untested.
5	Our supervised approaches (DeBERTa verifier, hidden state probes, MLP/RF on signal combinations) all fail to close the oracle gap; whether content-aware verifiers with access to task-specific structure could succeed remains open.
6	
7	We evaluate at $N{=}8$ (primary) and $N{=}16$; behavior at larger sample sizes is unknown (Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
8	The free-form ablation covers SciERC NER and CoNLL NER with one model; RE free-form results are in Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension}.
9	Qwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint used empty \texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks; a three-part analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:thinking_token}).
10	LP compression worsens on easy datasets at lower temperature (CoNLL tied fractions reach 95.8\% at $T{=}0.7$); whether reduced temperature translates to selection gains remains untested.
11	We tested verbalized confidence \cite{lin2022teaching, tian2023calibration} as a second per-sample signal; it exhibits severe overconfidence (mean 8.58/10 on SciERC, 9.24/10 on CoNLL) and yields the weakest QE and selection among all signals (Appendix~\ref{app:verbalized_confidence}).
12	Ad-hoc LP${\times}$VC combinations yield at most $+0.2$~pp over greedy (not significant; \S\ref{sec:discussion}); whether \textit{learned} combinations could close the gap remains open.
13	The $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ threshold used for tied/non-tied LP stratification is a post-hoc exploratory choice; while selection F1 is stable across $\varepsilon \in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}), the specific subset gains should be interpreted as characterizing the compression phenomenon rather than as a tuned selection strategy.
14	We tested one remedy for LP compression (entity-token-only LP, excluding format/schema tokens) which fails to improve selection despite increasing within-instance variance on SciERC ($4{\times}$); LP${\times}$VC combinations also fail to recover selection (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Our supervised signal combination baseline uses only the five unsupervised signals studied; a DeBERTa verifier encoding input-output pairs also fails to close the gap (preliminary, SciERC $n{=}106$), hidden state probes on the generating model's representations improve correlation ($+37$\% on SciERC) but do not improve selection (gap closure $-5.1$\%; Appendix~\ref{app:hidden_probe}), ; the bottleneck extends to model-internal access. Entity embeddings and calibration-based approaches remain untested.
15	Other per-sample scoring methods exist but were not evaluated: Self-Certainty \cite{kang2025selfcertainty} (consistency of token-level self-evaluation) and P(True) \cite{kadavath2022language} (model's own probability estimate of correctness) both produce per-sample scores and could in principle address the alignment bottleneck; whether they suffer from similar compression under fine-tuning is unknown.
16	Our selection experiments use 3 training seeds for Few-NERD and CoNLL (stability $\sigma{\leq}0.04$~pp for LP selection); these confirm robustness on the key positive and negative datasets. SciERC uses 1 primary seed with high seed sensitivity ($\sigma{=}0.48$~pp, $n{=}529$), which reflects limited within-seed statistical power.
17	Few-NERD models are trained for 3 epochs (vs.\ 5 for other datasets) due to earlier convergence; epoch confound analyses on both Few-NERD (2-point) and SciERC (3-point, including 10-epoch overfitting) are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}.
18	
```

| appendix.tex:231 | inline (同上) | LLaMA-Qwen degen差值 | +10.7pp | +10.4pp | 23.4% - 13.0% = 10.4% |
